### [1] [Terrain-Aware Thompson Sampling for Multi-Drone Search and Rescue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14685)
*Riana Gagnon Souleiman,Giovanni Beltrame*

Abstract: In this work, we propose a decentralized terrain-aware multi-drone Thompson Sampling algorithm tailored for search and rescue (SAR) applications. A probability distribution of locating a lost person is informed by terrain data, using established SAR models (displacement ring, elevation, watershed, dispersion angle, and track offset) derived from historical SAR case data on lost person behavior. Exploration is conducted by a team of drones using Thompson Sampling, where candidate actions are selected by drawing samples from this probability distribution, represented by a per-cell Gaussian posterior whose mean and variance are updated through Bayesian inference. This approach balances exploration of uncertain regions with exploitation of high-probability areas to locate a lost person. Observation uncertainty is incorporated through fractional forest coverage, which measures the percentage of an observation occluded by tree coverage and accounts for reduced detection performance in densely vegetated areas. Simulations using real SAR case data (YOSAR) evaluate the proposed method against a coverage planner and a probabilistic active search method in search regions of 1.5 and 3.8 square kilometers, with 5, 10, 15, and 20 agents flying at 50 km/hr. We demonstrate that our method achieves a success rate of approximately 87% within 60 to 247 seconds in the 1.5 square kilometer region, and success rates of approximately 75% to 91% within 161 to 466 seconds in the 3.8 square kilometer region, across different agent configurations.

</details>


### [2] [SunLightBots: Turning sunlight into self-sustained, high-power motion in soft robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.14729)
*Yoo Jin Lee,Melanie Torres Crespo,Zhongxiao Deng,Andrew Tejada-Vega,Rebecca Kramer-Bottiglio,Wenzhong Yan,Yimin Luo*

Abstract: Soft robots built from active materials can directly convert environmental stimuli into motion, offering a route to energy autonomy and embodied control. However, environmental energy is generally harvested at low power, whereas rapid, mechanically demanding actions require high mechanical output power. Reconciling this mismatch while sustaining self-regulated cyclic actuation is challenging, particularly at the subgram scale, where limited payload capacity constrains the integration of conventional power and control hardware. Here, we present SunLightBots, a class of subgram, sunlight-powered, untethered soft robots that achieve autonomous locomotion under continuous illumination near 1 Sun (100 mW/cm2). Their operation is enabled by a self-resetting snapping (SRS) mechanism that integrates liquid crystal elastomers (LCEs) with snap-through instability to accumulate solar energy supplied at low power, release it as rapid, high-power motion, and reset through photothermal-mechanical feedback. Through coordinated optimization of the LCE actuation temperature, photothermal conversion, actuation stress, and structural energy barrier, the system operates near 1 Sun and achieves a peak mass-specific mechanical power of 89 W/kg. At this irradiance, the optimized LCE generates an actuation stress of 0.1 MPa at approximately 60 deg C. SunLightBots demonstrate sustained swimming, crawling, and repeated impulsive jumping. Under 1.5 Suns, the swimmer advances more than three body lengths per cycle. This work provides a materials-mechanics route toward autonomous soft robots powered entirely by the environment.

</details>

Abstract: Prosthetic bypasses allow able-bodied individuals to walk with a prosthesis, enabling safe early-stage testing of new prosthetic devices before clinical trials. However, existing transtibial bypasses impose constraints, such as a contralateral shoe lift or a lateral offset of the prosthesis, and their effects on gait remain poorly documented. We present an open-source transtibial prosthetic bypass that preserves part of the sagittal knee mobility without these constraints. Six able-bodied participants familiarized themselves with the bypass for approximately 10~min, then walked on a treadmill with and without it, and joint kinematics were recorded with motion capture and then extracted with OpenSim. All participants were able to walk without handrail support, although single stance remained shorter on the bypass side (27% vs. 45% of the gait cycle). Bypass-side ankle kinematics were consistent with those reported for transtibial prosthesis users, whereas peak knee flexion during swing decreased from 61° to 36° (p<0.001) and hip range of the sound leg increased from 35° to 51° (p<0.01). Although gait with the bypass does not fully replicate that of prosthesis users, it yielded consistent gait patterns after only 10~min of learning. This bypass therefore provides a useful tool for the lower-limb prosthetics research community, facilitating preliminary experiments with able-bodied participants.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models have made significant progress in general-purpose robotic manipulation. However, sampled action sequences can be ineffective, while evaluating alternatives with computationally expensive verifiers adds latency at each policy query. In this work, we propose HDH, a lightweight framework that uses hindsight from a frozen VLA's own deployments to improve action verification. The framework has three stages. First, we deploy the policy and collect its autonomous rollouts. We then train a compact action verifier to assign each action chunk a predicted task-success score. Finally, the frozen VLA generates action candidates, and the verifier selects the candidate with the highest score for execution. Across four LIBERO-10 tasks, HDH achieves an average success rate of 82.3\% and on three real-world manipulation tasks, HDH improves average success over the baseline policy by 22.6\%.

</details>

Abstract: As social and service robots increasingly share interaction spaces in public, they may form groups while coordinating tasks or interacting with humans. Initial group formation alone is insufficient in dynamic settings, because it can be disrupted when a person or another agent joins, leaves, or passes through the group. Although group dynamics and repositioning have been widely studied in swarm robotics and virtual-agent settings, little attention has been given to socially appropriate repositioning behaviors in groups of humanoid robots. Existing social-force-based approaches provide a useful foundation for group behavior, but they have limited obstacle handling, and can produce unsafe behavior as environmental complexity increases. We propose a novel framework that modulates traditional Social Force, guided by Model Predictive Control (MPC) to plan ahead, bounded by Control Barrier Functions (CBF) to increase safety for robot groups repositioning behaviors. This approach aims to produce safer and socially aware group repositioning behaviors under various group formation disturbance. We evaluate the method in simulation through an ablation study and an online user study. The results show that our proposed method can produce smoother and safer repositioning behaviors in complex environments while preserving social appropriateness.

Abstract: Conventional vector-field-based motion planners for nonholonomic robots consist of two steps: first designing a nominal vector field and then controlling the robot to follow it to approach the target. However, a nonholonomic robot cannot follow the vector field instantaneously and may collide with obstacles, due to the inevitable mismatch between the robot's initial heading and the vector field's direction. This paper proposes a diffeomorphic vector-field alignment (DIVAL) method, which aligns the vector field's direction with the robot's initial heading so the robot can follow the vector field from the start. Specifically, the nominal vector field is locally reshaped by applying a diffeomorphic deformation to the workspace, such that the reshaped vector field has the same direction as the robot's heading at the initial position and coincides with the nominal vector field outside the deformed region. Since DIVAL eliminates the initial heading mismatch, the nonholonomic robot can maintain heading alignment with the reshaped vector field throughout the motion. This heading alignment further ensures that the robot preserves the nominal vector field's properties, including obstacle avoidance, convergence to the target, and smooth control signals. Numerical simulations provide vector-field-tracking baselines where the nominal vector fields are reshaped with DIVAL. Compared to the original baselines, the proposed method achieves collision-free convergence in all trials and avoids control discontinuities. Hardware experiments on a ground vehicle further demonstrate collision-free convergence across different targets and initial conditions under real-world disturbances.

</details>

Abstract: Model-based planners that roll out imagined futures assume the transition model faithfully propagates task-relevant signals---a property rarely measured directly. In a single-seed case study of a Recurrent State Space Model world model with active-inference planning in the CARLA driving simulator, we identify a \textbf{posterior-prior gap}---posterior (observation-filtered) states retain obstacle information that imagined rollout states progressively lose: a supervised obstacle probe achieves AUC 0.85 on posterior states, but the same probe applied to imagined prior states shows monotonic decay (AUC 0.85$\rightarrow$0.71 over 15 steps; logit separation drops 57\%). Because the probe is identical, the decay isolates the transition model---not the representation---as the information bottleneck. Obstacle avoidance drops to 0\% without privileged runtime coordinates, and the planner's own ensemble-based epistemic term does not flag the loss---ensemble disagreement yields AUC 0.52 as an obstacle classifier (chance level), making the failure an unflagged imagination failure---an unknown unknown at planning time. As a secondary contribution, we show that \textbf{task-conditioned weighting of Effective Free Energy components} over the same world model yields lane keeping ($81.2 \pm 1.8$\% route completion) and obstacle avoidance (28/28 avoided, \emph{using privileged coordinates}) in the CARLA simulator, with runtime weight modulation enabling post-evasion centering.

</details>

Abstract: The geometric understanding of a scene is a crucial component for safe and efficient robotic operations in novel and unstructured environments. The recent emergence of foundation models for Monocular Depth Estimation (MDE) has provided an opportunity to enrich robotic perception capabilities with dense long-range depth predictions, replacing expensive onboard hardware (e.g., LiDARs and stereo cameras). However, these offline models are primarily trained on affine-invariant depth representations, and their generalization to novel scenes remains unclear. In this work we present EM-ViT, a monocular depth estimation model that learns to adapt online relative depth inputs from a foundation model prior to the metric scale with observations collected during robotic operations. Motivated by the stereo camera parameterization, our method decomposes depth prediction via dense disparity refinement to recover the scene's relative structure and an effective baseline scalar prediction to determine the missing metric scale. The learning process leverages sparse LiDAR points and, optionally, the robot's Ego-Motion (EM) to rapidly train a lightweight Vision Transformer (ViT), while an internal gradient matching loss preserves the fine-grained semantic structure of the input disparity in real-time. We demonstrate the efficacy of our method on publicly available sequences, such as KITTI, and show that our online learning pipeline can adapt to novel scenes faster and with higher accuracy than existing MDE foundation models. The code and supplementary materials will be publicly available at https://emvit-mde.github.io.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models have emerged as a promising paradigm for general-purpose robotic manipulation. In our production setting, hundreds of VLA-powered robots are already operating in real-world environments, creating an immediate need for efficient fleet serving. We present XInfer, a production-deployed VLA serving system designed to reduce the inference infrastructure cost of large-scale robotic deployments. XInfer jointly coordinates heterogeneous VLA computation with the evolving response requirements of robotic execution. By exploiting computational parallelism while managing interference, XInfer improves GPU efficiency while protecting time-critical robotic feedback. We evaluate XInfer across three VLA models using production-derived workloads, achieving 1.26-3.75 times the effective robot capacity (ERC) of state-of-the-art systems under physical-execution constraints. Our implementation is publicly available at https://github.com/X-Square-Robot/xinfer.

Abstract: Extending humanoid interaction generation to new environments remains limited by the need for prebuilt 3D scene assets. Reconstructing scenes directly from single RGB images offers a way to overcome this limitation, but introduces a key challenge in maintaining geometric consistency throughout motion generation and transfer. Visually plausible human motions may be misaligned with the reconstructed scene, while retargeting can introduce additional support errors due to differences between human and robot embodiments.
  We present a unified pipeline that transforms a single RGB image into a humanoid motion reference. It reconstructs scene geometry from the input image, generates a human interaction conditioned on the reconstructed scene, and retargets the motion to a humanoid robot. To maintain geometric consistency throughout this process, we incorporate two complementary refinement stages before and after embodiment transfer. Before retargeting, Joint Metric Interaction Refinement (JMIR) refines human motion alignment with the registered metric scene while preserving the generated interaction structure. Following retargeting, Contact Guided Residual Retargeting (CGRR) corrects robot specific support errors to restore the intended support relationships. Together, these stages produce motion references that are consistent with the reconstructed scene and can be followed by multiple pretrained humanoid trackers without scene specific policy fine tuning. Compared with GRAIL, our method reduces refinement time from 480 s to 87 s, yielding a speedup of 5.5 times. The complete pipeline achieves a success rate of 89% in simulation and 70% across 50 physical trials on the Unitree G1, demonstrating its effectiveness in generating executable humanoid motion references from single RGB images.

</details>

Abstract: Humanoid motion tracking is fundamental to reproducing human motions on humanoid robots and serves as an important tool for teleoperation and motion data collection. However, directly tracking reference motions can be unsafe in constrained environments or during physical human--robot interaction, motivating the need for compliant motion tracking. We present ILA, an Interface-Level Adapter for compliant humanoid motion tracking without dedicated interaction sensors. ILA adapts motion references from the robot's observation history, allowing the robot to respond to physical guidance while preserving the capabilities of a pretrained motion tracker. As an interface-level module, ILA can be directly plugged into different pretrained motion trackers without retraining either the adapter or the tracking policies. ILA is trained entirely in simulation to predict reference corrections induced by physical interaction. We evaluate ILA with three humanoid motion trackers and achieve a 63.6--75.4% reduction in guidance-target error compared with their original policies. Matched simulation studies and hardware experiments further show that ILA improves responsiveness to human guidance while preserving nominal motion-tracking performance. These results demonstrate compliant guidance as a reusable, sensor-free extension to pretrained humanoid motion trackers.

</details>

Abstract: Imitation policies can perform complex manipulation, yet small execution errors can accumulate and cause failure. Refinement can correct these errors, but existing approaches often require additional on-policy learning or costly online planning. Our key insight is that a pretrained policy already supplies a useful nominal behavior: refinement can focus on predicting how small action corrections change its outcome. We therefore learn this local response in a form that is cheap to optimize. Our \textit{action-affine world model} (A\textsuperscript{2}WM) learns local action sensitivities offline from action-perturbed policy rollouts. Given interaction history and a policy proposal, it predicts task-relevant geometry as a nominal trajectory plus a linear response to corrections. It predicts the full planning horizon directly, avoiding the propagation of intermediate errors through recursive model evaluations. Quadratic penalties on predicted task errors and linear correction constraints then yield a cheap convex quadratic program (QP). Online refinement requires one model evaluation and one QP solve, while both policy and model weights remain frozen. Across four simulated manipulation tasks, our framework achieves the highest success rate among evaluated baselines, adding only 3.8 ms of mean refinement overhead per control step, excluding base-policy sampling.

</details>

Abstract: Embodied world models (WMs) are implicit learning systems that predict the next future latent state directly from observations and actions. Unique from other end-to-end learning systems, the dynamics predictor of WMs can simulate the environment's response to alternative actions without physical deployment nor pre-specifying states of the dynamical system. Current metrics focus on the quality of model outputs, e.g., clarity of reconstructions or task success, but fail to assess the fidelity of modeling consequences of counterfactual actions. Inspecting the forced response to action perturbations through sensitivity analysis is one way to audit how well WMs can predict the consequences of counterfactual actions. Deploying WMs on hardware will require new evaluation frameworks to analyze the tradeoffs between state error and accurate sensitivity prediction.

</details>

Abstract: 6-DOF pose estimation of large and heavy industrial objects, such as large piping systems in shipyards and structural components in petrochemical plants, remains relatively underexplored in robotic perception. Repeatedly repositioning large objects is physically and spatially demanding, making it difficult to acquire real training data with sufficient pose diversity. Synthetic data offer a scalable alternative, but faithfully reproducing real RGB-D observations, particularly the structured measurement errors of real depth sensors, remains difficult. We propose MiniPose, a practical pipeline for miniature-based real RGB-D data collection that addresses both the data-acquisition burden and the sim-to-real gap. We fabricate a scaled-down replica and capture RGB-D data of it in diverse poses using a real RGB-D camera. RGB images are used directly, while depth maps are converted to a geometrically consistent representation of the full-scale object via a simple inverse scaling transformation. This allows training to incorporate real appearance and sensor measurement characteristics that are difficult to reproduce using synthetic data alone. To examine whether the benefit depends on a particular network, we evaluate two RGB-D pose estimators with different formulations, PVN3D and RDPN6D. Mixing at most 1,000 miniature images per pipe type, less than 10\% of the training data, with 10,000 synthetic images improves the mean ADD-S AUC across pipe types over synthetic-only training for both estimators. A comparison with training on full-scale real data further suggests that miniature data can serve as a proxy where full-scale collection is difficult, reducing the data-collection burden for large industrial objects.

</details>

Abstract: Real-world robot imitation learning is often limited by the cost of collecting physical demonstrations, which typically yield only one action label per visited state. We present CrowdMAPS, a web-based crowdsourcing paradigm for augmenting real-robot datasets with Multiple Actions per State (MAPS). After or during standard robot data collection, CrowdMAPS distributes visited robot states to remote annotators through a web-based 3D interface, where they specify alternative valid actions without requiring access to physical robot hardware. This expands robot datasets along a complementary "width" dimension--by increasing the number of actions per visited state--rather than only by collecting more trajectories. We evaluate CrowdMAPS using keyframe-centric goal-pose annotations supplied by non-expert annotators and train imitation policies on the resulting MAPS data. Across five real-world manipulation tasks, increasing MAPS width from one to ten actions per state improves sample efficiency and increases average success rates by 35.6 percentage points for keypose policies and 33.0 percentage points for trajectory policies. These results show that software-mediated human annotations can augment physical robot data to afford denser supervision, establishing CrowdMAPS as a scalable digital layer for real-robot data collection.

</details>

Abstract: Open-world navigation requires a robot to locate a user-specified target in an indoor scene under open-vocabulary queries. Realistic indoor environments are semi-static: walls and furniture largely persist while smaller objects move, relocate, or vanish between visits. Prior-map systems answer queries from object poses recorded at reconstruction time, which expire as soon as objects move, whereas online methods re-derive where to look from current observations every few steps, often through expensive calls to a vision--language model. Assuming that walls and large furniture do not move, we keep their map to plan paths, but use a target--furniture co-occurrence prior to decide where to search. We present PreNav, an ObjectNav framework on semi-static environments. Rather than invoking a vision-language model at each step, PreNav generates a single global search reference before the robot moves: an LLM converts target--furniture co-occurrence into a spatial prior, a conditional diffusion model turns that prior and the persistent structure into an ordered centerline with a band around it, and the robot approaches the target once it is detected online. Experiments on a Gibson ObjectNav benchmark show that PreNav outperforms a strong online baseline under matched structural priors, using a generator trained only on room-level ScanNet++ scenes and applied to floor-level Gibson without fine-tuning. Real-world experiments under object relocation and open-vocabulary queries further validate the approach.

</details>

Abstract: When robots interact with elastic surfaces such as mattresses and trampolines, changes in loading cause the surfaces to deform and rebound, altering subsequent contact locations and forces. We present Elastra, a simulation framework for modeling this interaction and supporting the training and evaluation of robot control policies. Elastra represents each surface using elements that move only vertically, with elastic connections and damping to model deformation and recovery. Contact forces drive surface motion, and the updated surface geometry is used to compute subsequent contact forces. We systematically validate our simulations against an independent implementation and a real mattress load curve. We further use Elastra to train the Unitree G1 humanoid to stand up on mattresses and trampolines. Using the pretrained HoST policy and ProtoMotions motion tracker as original controllers, we train residual policies through reinforcement learning to add corrections to their joint targets. Adding these residual policies increases the success rate with HoST from 44.1\% to 72.5\%, and from 55\% to 89\% on the trampoline. Abstract: Quadrupedal mobile manipulators can extend their manipulation workspace by coordinating the mounted arm with the mobile base. At the task level, short-horizon end-effector (EE) trajectories provide an interface for specifying the desired manipulation motion without prescribing how it should be realized by the arm and base. However, executing such trajectories remains challenging because the controller must determine when and how the base should move, and this decision depends on the mounted-arm morphology. We therefore present PolyMorphWBC, a hierarchical controller that takes an EE trajectory as the sole task-level motion command and generates coordinated arm--base motions across four families of 6-DoF mounted arms, with extended-reach trajectories beyond the initial fixed-base reach. Within PolyMorphWBC, a trajectory-conditioned coordinator determines the base motion from previewed EE targets, while arm-specific Inverse Kinematics (IK) tracks the current target, and a frozen locomotion backend executes the resulting motion. Cross-arm reuse is enabled by a Unified Robot Description Format (URDF)-derived morphology conditioning, together with online residual-wrench feedback. We train PolyMorphWBC on procedurally generated arms and structured trajectories within and beyond fixed-base reach. In IsaacLab, it achieves 93.95% completion with 2.30cm position error and 4.81° orientation error on held-out trajectories and arm morphologies. In MuJoCo evaluation against UMI-on-Legs, PolyMorphWBC reduces EE position error by 69--72% and orientation error by 91--97% across four within- and extended-reach trajectories. On a physical Go2--ARX5 system, we demonstrate language-conditioned extended-reach pick-and-place using EE trajectories from pi_0 without vision-language-action (VLA)-generated base commands.

Abstract: Automated driving has gained remarkable momentum over the past decade, with both industry and academia investing substantial resources in developing perception and control solutions to advance vehicle autonomy. However, these efforts have largely focused on clear-weather scenarios, leaving harsh conditions, such as snow, rain, and fog, comparatively understudied. As a result, inclement weather remains one of the most significant hurdles for autonomous vehicles, often leading to substantial performance degradation in downstream perception and control tasks.
  In this paper, we present insights from the EU-funded Horizon Europe project ROADVIEW, which aims to develop robust, weather-aware perception and control modules for connected and automated vehicles operating under severe conditions, including snow, rain, and fog. Drawing on four years of large-scale development and real-world demonstrations across multiple vehicle platforms in different locations, we identify ten key challenges that hinder the development of weather-resilient autonomy, including data scarcity, domain shifts, limitations in multimodal perception, control robustness, and validation gaps, among others. We summarize these challenges and lessons learned toward enabling reliable snow-autonomous driving, highlighting critical directions for future research and standardization.

Abstract: We present DroneDiffusion, a perception-aware diffusion planner for local 3-D UAV trajectory generation that explicitly models and avoids failure zones caused by lens flare, overexposure, and reflective surfaces. A vision-language perceptual safety estimator produces a pixel-aligned reliability map that highlights regions of degraded sensing. A denoising diffusion probabilistic model (DDPM) is conditioned on this map together with the onboard RGB image, goal point, and initial velocity to generate egocentric 3-D trajectories. To enforce obstacle avoidance at inference time without retraining, we apply training-free CHOMP guidance over a 3-D Euclidean signed distance field. The model is trained entirely in simulation and deployed zero-shot on a real drone. Reliability conditioning raises the mean perceptual reliability along generated trajectories to 0.71, compared with 0.63 for a geometric baseline, while guidance halves the collision rate of the learned prior from 14.7% to 7.8%. Perceptual safety conditioning and geometric guidance are therefore complementary: treating sensor reliability as a first-class signal reduces incursions into unreliable regions while maintaining competitive navigation performance.

</details>

Abstract: Navigating off-road trails on a mountain bike requires diverse locomotion skills, including aerial maneuvers, cornering, and slip recovery, paired with sophisticated perception for scene understanding. Translating these capabilities to autonomous, two-wheeled robots remains a formidable challenge. We show that autonomous, high-speed trail navigation can be achieved through a single idea: encoding the trail as a semantic layer of the terrain. In simulation, this layer embeds the navigation task into the training environment; during deployment, it is reconstructed onboard from camera and LiDAR data. First, we introduce a unified learning framework that treats local navigation and locomotion as a joint optimization problem, bypassing the twist or pose command interface of classical control hierarchies. With fixed reward weights, the same framework produces both trail racing and agile parkour, shaped only through terrain semantics and episode length. Second, our perception pipeline combines a frozen vision foundation model with a lightweight dual-head decoder that predicts per-pixel terrain semantics and confidence scores, which are fused with LiDAR range measurements into an egocentric semantic elevation map at 30 Hz onboard an NVIDIA AGX Orin. We validate our framework on the Ultra Mobility Vehicle (UMV), a bicycle robot that autonomously raced across all sections of a kilometer-long, unstructured mountain bike trail at speeds exceeding 5 m/s and averaging 3.26 m/s. In simulation, our policy fails up to an order of magnitude less often than hierarchical baselines while driving faster.

Abstract: Vision-based high-speed navigation requires a trajectory planner that can reactively generate smooth, collision-free trajectories from onboard visual observations. Deep reinforcement learning offers a promising approach to vision-based trajectory planning, as it enables the optimization of sequential decision-making policies in complex environments. However, model-free policy gradient methods rely on sample-based policy gradient estimation, which yields noisy gradient estimates and inefficient training. To this end, we propose a framework for learning trajectory planning policies using the analytic policy gradient (APG) method and use it to train APG-Planner, a vision-based high-speed trajectory planner. The proposed framework leverages differential flatness to analytically reconstruct vehicle states from planned trajectories, thereby enabling differentiable trajectory-level state transitions without requiring an explicit low-level dynamics model. By iteratively performing trajectory planning and differentiable state transitions, we construct a differentiable episode rollout and analytically compute policy gradients from episode-level losses, improving both training efficiency and policy performance. After approximately 30 minutes of training on a single GPU, APG-Planner demonstrates comparable or superior performance to existing baseline methods in simulation and achieves speeds of up to 11\,m/s in real-world flight experiments. We further validate the proposed framework on a skid-steer ground robot, demonstrating its applicability to differentially flat robotic systems beyond quadrotors.

Abstract: Bimanual robotic manipulation is versatile, yet its central challenge lies not only in executing dexterous motion with each hand, but also in coordinating the two arms for long-horizon manipulation under coupled spatial and temporal constraints. Prior work has largely focused on endowing robots with human-level manipulation skills, paying less attention to the structure of bimanual behaviour itself: many practical tasks are a coordinated composition of reusable unimanual skills and a small set of synchronous bimanual skills. This makes high-level spatio-temporal coordination, rather than skill acquisition alone, a key bottleneck for efficient bimanual execution. We propose BiPOP, a framework that couples Large Language Models (LLMs) with Temporal Partial-Order Planning for long-horizon bimanual manipulation. At its core is an explicit spatio-temporal model that partitions the workspace by arm reachability and captures inter-arm dependencies, arm assignment, synchronization requirements, and opportunities for safe concurrent execution. Building on this model, an LLM translates natural-language instructions into a formal PDDL specification, while a temporal planner enforces the constraints and schedules actions, yielding plans that are logically consistent at the symbolic level and efficient through parallel execution. On simulated long-horizon tasks of varying complexity, BiPOP outperforms open-loop planning with strong reasoning models and closed-loop ReAct-style planning in success rate, planning time, and plan efficiency, and the full pipeline runs end to end on the real bimanual robot NICOL. A failure analysis further shows that LLM-only planners mainly violate spatial and temporal coordination constraints, which motivates delegating these constraints to a symbolic planner. Code: https://github.com/Kchu/BiPOP

</details>

Abstract: Granular materials exhibit complex and highly variable interaction dynamics, making their reliable manipulation challenging without knowledge of their physical properties. We present an interactive perception framework that infers granular material properties directly from force-torque measurements acquired during scooping. The proposed Latent Filter++ combines a bio-inspired frequency-temporal filter-bank representation with a causal latent dynamical model, allowing the representation to evolve as interaction observations become available while avoiding hand-crafted signal preprocessing. The learned latent representation captures physically meaningful variation in granular materials and supports estimation of properties including grain size and bulk density. Evaluated on 26 diverse granular materials, the approach generalizes to materials excluded from training and enables few-shot adaptation of property-specific mappings using a small number of additional interaction samples. Finally, we demonstrate the relevance of the inferred properties to downstream manipulation by examining their correlation with the maximum acceleration the materials can withstand without spilling from the spoon during transport.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) can improve robot learning by jointly predicting future visual states and actions, but generating a visual future at every replanning step is expensive and sometimes unnecessary. This raises a critical question: when should a robot policy imagine the future? In this paper, we propose Bind-WAM, a binding-uncertainty-guided WAM for selective future imagination. Bind-WAM introduces language-object binding uncertainty, selectively invoking joint future-action generation when the current observation does not support stable task-relevant action generation. An offline labeling pipeline uses complete demonstrations and action context to identify task-relevant objects and detect reference mismatch, invisibility, and identity drift. The resulting pseudo-labels supervise a lightweight router that predicts binding uncertainty from the current observation and language instruction. At inference, a shared WAM policy invokes selective future imagination according to the predicted uncertainty and its change between consecutive replanning steps. Simulation and real-world experiments demonstrate that Bind-WAM can establish binding uncertainty as a strong, interpretable signal for selective future imagination.

</details>

Abstract: Industrial manipulators offer reach and precision but can damage nearby structures in confined spaces. Soft robots provide compliant contact but typically have limited reach. Rigid-soft systems combine their advantages, although they often require dedicated actuation and control hardware. We propose actuating a passive tendon-driven soft extension through manipulator joint motion. As the robot moves, the exterior spans of tendons anchored to upstream links change, altering tendon tension and deforming the extension. Inspection through narrow apertures, for example, constrains the motion of the end effector (EE). We develop a controller that regulates the soft tip while preserving such EE constraints. A differential model separates rigid transport from soft deformation, combining analytic arm and tendon kinematics with a locally identified span-to-deformation map. We also derive a local upper bound on the number of independent deformation directions transmitted by the tendons under the EE constraint, and conditions for the exponential convergence of the tip error. A quadratic program with control barrier functions imposes obstacle clearance, fulcrum limits, and model-validity conditions. Simulations and real experiments show tip and shape regulation under free, position-constrained, and fulcrum-constrained EE motion.

Abstract: Existing methods for predicting surrounding vehicles' forward occupancy may under-approximate the ground-truth occupancy, exposing the ego vehicle to collision risk, or produce overly conservative regions that unnecessarily restrict feasible space and cause planning failures. To address these issues, we propose FloCOS, a conditional flow-matching framework that predicts compact forward occupancy sets with high occupancy coverage. FloCOS represents the forward occupancy set as a time-varying union of half-space convex polygons parameterized by temporal basis coefficients. Conditional flow matching captures the conditional distribution of these coefficients to govern the temporal evolution of the convex components, trained under coverage and union-area losses that penalize missed occupancy and excess area. For downstream trajectory optimization, the predicted nonconvex union is directly incorporated into hyperplane-based collision-avoidance constraints. Evaluations on the Waymo Open Motion Dataset show that FloCOS generates compact forward occupancy sets with high coverage and achieves a higher interaction completion rate than evaluated baselines. Real-world vehicle experiments further confirm its ability to support collision-free planning in interactive scenarios.

</details>

Abstract: Most action tokenizers for autoregressive vision language action (VLA) models compress motion into chunks that must be decoded in full before their actions become executable. We introduce ActionCodec2, a non-neural streaming tokenizer that releases actions incrementally while keeping trajectory reconstruction error within a prescribed tolerance. Its encoder quantizes each step relative to the decoder's reconstructed state, so the selected motion primitive corrects residual error rather than allowing errors from independently quantized increments to accumulate. Finding the smallest such primitive set is a geometric covering problem; we derive the exact minimum for translation and constant-factor-tight bounds for rotation. Set-BPE then losslessly merges co-occurring primitive symbols across coordinates and neighboring steps, without a fixed coordinate ordering. A universal version fitted on 20k hours of data from 24 embodiments averages 8.8 tokens per second for 15 Hz end-effector motion on this corpus. With the same VLA backbone, ActionCodec2 outperforms the compared tokenizers on three simulation benchmarks and in zero-shot real-robot evaluation. Its streaming representation allows policies to incorporate state feedback within an action chunk, substantially improving performance over open-loop execution under randomized dynamics, external disturbances, and moving objects. The same tokens also can serve as a direct control interface for VLM agents, which compose successful action sequences into reusable macros. These agent-built macros reduce control decisions and thinking-token usage across attempts and transfer to a held-out task. More results are available at https://zibindong.github.io/actioncodec2-page/

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models predict chunks of future actions, and when objects move, those chunks go stale before they are executed. We study what makes a pretrained VLA reactive, using \pihalf{} on two dynamic simulation benchmarks and three real-robot tasks. We vary the prediction and execution horizons, compare synchronous and asynchronous inference, test methods that deliver actions sooner or keep successive chunks continuous, and adapt the policy with motion cues and latent-space reinforcement learning. The best prediction horizon is about one second, and the best execution horizon depends on the checkpoint. Short horizons help more with asynchronous execution, and methods that keep the trajectory continuous outperform those that only reduce latency. Adapting the policy repairs targeted failures, but on the conveyor task changing the execution horizon gained as much. Reactivity depends on the execution pipeline as much as on the policy, so both should be evaluated together.

</details>

Existing methods support multi-scale navigation through rich environment representations, but at substantial computational and storage cost. These costs limit deployment where wireless coverage is incomplete and robots must rely on onboard computation to keep navigating. We introduce EMS-Nav, a biologically inspired multi-scale navigation framework for online, on-device operation. Its core innovation is to organize perception, spatial understanding, and navigation around regions, making local spaces the basic units of both environmental understanding and computation. EMS-Nav groups objects and their relationships into functionally meaningful spaces and builds a hierarchy from local areas to the broader environment. This organization supports efficient map construction alongside the interpretation and execution of language goals at different scales. In multi-scale navigation experiments, EMS-Nav achieves the highest success rates across all four target categories: objects, rooms, regions, and specific instances. It outperforms state-of-the-art open-vocabulary mapping and navigation methods, improving overall first-attempt success by 9.39 percentage points over the strongest baseline while reducing mapping overhead and language-model input size. Local deployment in real indoor environments further demonstrates its applicability to on-device embodied navigation. By establishing regions as the organizing basis for perception, spatial understanding, and language-guided action, EMS-Nav provides a system that combines multi-scale understanding with computational efficiency to support diverse language goals on resource-constrained robots.

Abstract: Endoscopic submucosal dissection (ESD) is a minimally invasive approach for removing large polyps from the colon and offers many benefits compared to other surgical approaches. However, ESD is technically very challenging to perform, leading to limited adoption by physicians. We propose the Helping Hand, a two-segment continuum robotic grasper that, together with an electrosurgical knife, enables tissue traction and cutting within a single 3.7 mm working channel. We achieve this functionality by creating a slot at the base of the Helping Hand that lets the electrosurgical knife extend out, enabling countertraction and cutting through one instrument lumen. We evaluate the Helping Hand's workspace and its ability to provide traction forces from different starting configurations. Finally, we demonstrate the full workflow by isolating and removing tissue in an ex vivo chicken thigh, illustrating the potential for the Helping Hand to help improve ESD procedures.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models are regarded as the cognitive foundation of embodied intelligence, but planning from instructions alone cannot make them reliable. During execution, actions deviate from intended effects, constrained by the embodiment and environment. An embodied brain must understand why execution deviates, identify what can be reliably realized, and revise decisions using interaction experience. These correspond to causal failure localization, capability-constrained adaptation, and experience-driven revision. Current embodied foundation models largely lack these capabilities. To address this, we introduce Embodied-Corrector. We construct failure-recovery data by injecting perturbations at subtask boundaries of successful trajectories, replaying affected subtasks, and keeping failures that are observable from public evidence and recoverable from the current state. We align actions, consequences, failure causes, and recoveries into Judge-Cause-Sub-Fine supervision. Training uses evaluation-first SFT: the model learns Judge and Cause first, then Sub and Fine conditioned on accurate prefixes. Since deployment uses self-generated prefixes, evaluation errors propagate into recovery planning, and response-level rewards are too coarse to locate affected planning tokens. We therefore use OPD to internalize the planning distribution under accurate prefixes into that under self-generated ones: the model generates plans from self-generated prefixes, while a contrastive forward pass with accurate prefixes compares token probabilities to reinforce or suppress planning tokens. At deployment, this branch is removed. Experiments show stronger failure analysis and corrective planning: with a frozen VLA, composite task success improves by 6.1 percentage points; with fixed GPT-5.5 execution, manipulation and navigation tasks improve by 13.9 percentage points.

</details>

Abstract: In active object search, a robot must decide where to look and when to stop. Imperfect detection complicates both decisions: a non-detection may indicate occlusion, an out-of-view target, or a false negative, while a detection may be a false positive. This raises the question of which representations a world model needs to support reliable search under imperfect detection. We introduce Bayesian Active Miss-aware Object Search (BAMOS) to investigate how target belief, occupancy belief, and detector errors affect search decisions. Through comprehensive ablations in Isaac Sim and HSSD houses, we examine the roles of these representations and their update mechanisms across different detector configurations. Notably, target belief and its update mechanisms are critical to search performance, with their contribution generally increasing as detection errors become more prevalent. These findings highlight the importance of maintaining and updating target beliefs to support reliable active search under uncertain detector observations, providing insights into the design of world models for embodied agents. Code is available in https://github.com/gokyeongryeol/BAMOS.

</details>

Abstract: Robot mechanics can bias kinematic measures of recovery and limit comparisons across rehabilitation devices. We developed a closed-loop human-robot simulation calibrated to published healthy and stroke group kinematics. Paired runs held motor-control parameters and movement plans fixed to quantify bias in seven metrics relative to a low-impedance reference, normalized using external repeatability benchmarks. Within a subset informed by published device specifications, median log dimensionless jerk (LDLJ) bias was 0.93 and 1.79 benchmark units for stroke and healthy profiles, respectively. Sobol analysis identified Coulomb friction and apparent mass as leading contributors; LDLJ bias persisted across 11 robustness variants. LDLJ bias increased as simulated movements became less fragmented. If recovery similarly reduces fragmentation, this effect could partly mask improvement in measured smoothness. Correction combining identified robot dynamics and observed metrics with gradient-boosted trees reduced median normalized absolute error from 0.77 to 0.10 for LDLJ and from 0.24 to 0.08 for spectral arc length (SPARC) in the device-informed subset of a random 20% hold-out. Median smoothness residuals remained at or below 0.15 benchmark units across four separately held-out parameter regions. Under an alternative controller, LDLJ error decreased from 1.60 to 0.17 without retraining, although transfer varied across metrics. These simulation results support mechanical characterization and movement-dependent correction toward more comparable robotic assessments, motivating validation in patients.

Abstract: Autonomous navigation of mobile robots in crowded environments, while preserving pedestrians' psychological comfort, requires distinguishing pedestrians from other obstacles, predicting their future motion, and accounting for uncertainty in the predicted trajectories of both pedestrians and the robot. Abstract: Full-size humanoid robots are being considered for hazardous industrial work such as disassembling electric-vehicle batteries. We describe the safety mechanisms of an open-source autonomy stack for the Unitree H1-2 humanoid and what deploying them taught us. All joint commands pass through a safety layer that runs separately from the control software. It enforces joint position, velocity, and torque limits, and it stops the robot when a state estimate becomes stale or when the hard-wired emergency stop (e-stop) is pressed or disconnected. We pushed the standing robot until it lost balance. With a model-predictive lower-body controller it never recovered, and the operator had to press the e-stop in every trial. Pressing it removes balance control, and the robot falls. The safety layer's own limits trigger the same stop, and limits chosen for manipulation were exceeded during normal walking. Near an operator or a battery pack, the fall can be more dangerous than the cause of the stop. Joint limits bound how much force a robot can apply, but not when applying it is appropriate. Drawing on research on responsibility and on child and adolescent development, we therefore make a case for granting robots force authority in stages, with a person or organization explicitly responsible for its use.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have demonstrated strong performance in robotic manipulation, yet most rely primarily on 2D visual observations and language instructions, leaving scene geometry and obstacle relationships to be inferred implicitly. This limitation is particularly problematic in cluttered environments, where small spatial errors can lead to unintended contacts or collisions. We propose MOSAIC, a modular spatial- and obstacle-aware framework that can be integrated with pretrained VLA policies to improve object-level scene understanding and action generation. Our method augments the VLA backbone with an auxiliary semantic grounding task that predicts role-specific masks for the target object, destination region, and surrounding obstacles. We further combine the target and destination masks with depth estimated from 3D scene reconstruction to construct role-specific geometric tokens that are injected directly into the action expert. In addition, we propose obstacle-aware attention supervision that emphasizes obstacles near the robot body during task execution. The resulting attention features are subsequently injected into later action-expert layers to further guide action generation. Together, these components provide explicit semantic, geometric, and obstacle-aware cues while preserving the underlying pretrained VLA architecture. We evaluate our framework on pretrained VLA model using the RoboPRO benchmark, including visual perturbation tests, and further validate it on real-world manipulation tasks. MOSAIC improves the success rate by 5.1% in cluttered scenes, and by 9.7% under visual perturbations, with gains of up to 25.0% in the most challenging environment. Abstract: This work addresses the problem of finding a concealed object in an environment by unifying formalisms for partially observable environments with multi-modal priors drawn from a mix of ontologies, semantic networks, LLMs, and VLMs. We propose a concealment-adaptive mixed-observability MDP (CA-MOMDP), a Bayes-adaptive setting where uncertainty is over both object locations and which nodes can conceal them, held as a hypothesis with a bounded ontology prior and resolved only by interaction. We define a hierarchical transition system (HTS) to abstract the environment into room and object nodes that can be planned over via our CA-MOMDP. We further propose a COMBS-score: a multi-modal fusion of a human informed semantic network, LLM, and VLM, to initialize the belief over object locations, which grounds our abstractions and reduces reliance on a single model. Belief is maintained via Bayes' rule, which never lets a look rule out a closed container. We prove a present target is found in a bounded number of searches under exact perception and that its location is never ruled out given missed detection under noisy perception. We ablate each component to demonstrate their significance. Across five simulator configurations, from a gridworld to Matterport3D with a real detector, COMBS finds 92-100% of in-distribution and 92-100% of out-of-distribution targets, matches or beats the baselines in processing speed, significantly outperforms a per-step LLM policy at lower cost on the larger scenes, recovers more out-of-distribution placements than a TidyBot ranking, and matches an LLM-only prior at a fraction of its per-decision latency. COMBS is the only standalone method that can declare a target absent at a stated error level, and the per-step LLM policy offered a termination action abandons 12-22% of targets that were present, against 1.6-3.2% for COMBS on the same episodes.

Reusing experience changes how the model acts but is not a free lunch, and scene geometry helps most, even when depth is predicted from RGB alone. Abstract: Motion imitation enables humanoid robots to acquire whole-body skills such as walking and dancing. Abstract: Multi-camera observations in off-road environments vary significantly in reliability, posing challenges when complementing degraded geometric constraints. Motivated by this, we propose Quality-aware View Selection Lidar-Vision Odometry Risk Mapping (QVS-LIVO RiskMap), which decouples visual processing for state estimation from that for semantic mapping. Channel health memory and switching hysteresis govern selection of at most one eligible view for direct photometric correction. The posterior is retained when no view is eligible or the correction fails acceptance. The mapping back end integrates semantic observations from all available views in a shared world grid. Vehicle-scale geometry constrains semantic risk corrections, while spatial and temporal evidence supports risk confirmation. The system also produces a local bird's-eye-view (BEV) risk map and a global 2.5D semantic-terrain risk map with obstacle instance voxels. On two field sequences, QVS-LIVO achieved horizontal root mean square errors of 0.910 m and 1.664 m. It reduced error by 43.3% relative to FAST-LIO2 on the degraded long-route sequence. When deployed onboard an NVIDIA Jetson AGX Orin, the system generates local and global maps at 9.94 Hz and 2.00 Hz, respectively, with a median local mapping latency of 32.4 ms. These runtime results demonstrate the real-time feasibility of selective visual state updates alongside multi-view semantic mapping under challenging off-road conditions. Abstract: Many robotic applications involve scenarios where multiple robots interact and the decisions of one robot influence the behavior of others. This paper presents a quadratic programming (QP)-based feedback controller for multi-robot conflict resolution with input constraints. Safety is enforced using coupled Control Barrier Function (CBF), while a yielding matrix encodes how robots influence each other. Unlike existing methods with fixed or hard priorities, the proposed framework enables yielding preferences while guaranteeing deadlock-free operation by design. Sufficient conditions are established for the controller to be locally Lipschitz. The optimal CBF sign- indefinite slack is shown to admit a finite uniform bound, which is compensated through an enlarged CBF safety margin to obtain a forward invariant set contained within the desired safe set. The analysis also identifies an irreducible slack component due to finite maximum deceleration that cannot be eliminated by increasing the slack penalty. Finally, sufficient conditions on the yielding matrix and nominal controller are derived to exclude deadlock states. Simulations demonstrate adaptive coordination across yielding preferences and robustness to robot failures.

</details>

Abstract: We study robot navigation in indoor environments from fixed exocentric cameras (infrastructure-mounted, external to the robot). We show that standard Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), which succeeds with onboard cameras, fails to learn this navigation end-to-end in our setup. This failure results from the perspective mismatch between exocentric observations and egocentric action and goal formulations, which is too complex to resolve from the reward signal alone. To address this, we propose Viewpoint-Invariant Navigation from Exocentric Cameras (VINE). VINE uses an obstacle map estimation network, trained with supervised learning, that takes exocentric images, camera pose, robot pose, and room dimensions as input and produces a viewpoint-invariant obstacle map. This map can be used either by a PPO policy or by a planner such as A*. VINE's map estimation performs better than perspective mapping approaches, handles cameras placed across a wide range of positions and orientations without view-specific tuning, requires only approximate camera pose estimates (noise with a standard deviation of 30cm per axis in x, y, and z and about 20° of rotation error on average), is robust to intrinsic variation, and extends to two-camera setups with partial coverage. Despite operating from cameras randomized in position during evaluation, VINE surpasses the navigation success rate of PPO with an onboard camera in simulated environments.

</details>

Abstract: Robots handle much more than rigid objects: towels, cables, water, sand. The simulators used to train robots at scale are strongest on rigid bodies, and adding another material usually means a second engine and hand-written glue between the two. We present Alloy, an open-source physics engine for robot learning in which rigid robots, cloth, cables, liquids, granular material and soft bodies share one scene. Each object is described once; Alloy assigns it to a solver suited to its material, couples the solvers inside every control step, and runs the scene in many parallel worlds on one GPU, or on a CPU. Every world carries a health grade, to help tell a failure of the physics from a failure of the policy. Alloy is built on Newton, MuJoCo Warp and Warp, and it is written to be driven by coding agents: its documentation ships inside the package, every error names its fix, and the engine is exposed as tools over the Model Context Protocol. In a cold test, agents that had never seen Alloy installed a pre-release from the package index and trained policies for walking, cable handling and pouring on a single GPU. We also report how throughput grows with the number of worlds, depth-camera throughput, agreement with closed-form references, and a reliability test over a thousand worlds.

</details>

Abstract: At disaster sites and on unstable slopes, robot contact can collapse uninspected terrain, requiring retreat upon support loss. Massively parallel reinforcement learning of collapse-reactive retreat requires independent state--action experiences. However, on shared rigid-body collapsible terrain, one robot's collapse alters others' contacts without direct interaction. We call this indirect interference. Duplicating terrain objects prevents this but is memory-intensive, limiting parallel sampling. Our key insight is that policy learning does not necessarily require rigid-body collapse modeling, which is feasible but computationally expensive; collapse-induced contact responses can suffice. We therefore propose a memory-efficient parallel policy-learning framework for collapse-reactive retreat via hybrid terrain modeling. Hybrid terrain modeling combines rigid-body simulation of non-collapsible terrain with virtual contact forces for collapsible regions. A State-Multiplexed Contact Model shares terrain geometry and a contact-response law while maintaining environment-specific collapse states, enabling independent experiences without terrain objects for collapsible regions. The framework trains a proprioceptive policy for both normal locomotion and collapse-reactive retreat. It supports over 100 times as many parallel environments as fine-resolution rigid-block terrain within the same GPU memory limit. This scalability and improved sample efficiency enable over 50 times faster policy convergence with comparable retreat performance. Sim-to-sim and real-robot experiments demonstrate retreat under multiple collapse conditions. A supplementary video is available at: https://youtu.be/_RrhwjzEPE0

</details>

Abstract: Reusing a demonstrated gait can reduce the work of specifying the same motion for each biped. A video captures the timing of a step, but each robot must execute it with its own limbs and actuators. MotionSkill recovers a timed reference once from monocular video and adapts it to each target in simulation. It aligns root motion and body landmarks in a common frame and records which landmarks are available at each instant. At each source timestamp, the mapper preserves limb directions, uses the target segment lengths, and solves inverse kinematics under joint limits and closure constraints. In MuJoCo, we train a separate residual policy for each of ten bipeds on its mapped reference from one four-second walking clip. Each policy uses the measured robot state to correct joint commands during contact. From initial states held out during training, MotionSkill completed 98% of trials on the original video and 26% with the upper body occluded for one second.

Abstract: Recent advances in robotic manipulation have enabled increasingly complex and dexterous behaviors, yet existing benchmarks still focus on simple pick-and-place tasks and lack a systematic framework for measuring progress in robot learning along the axis of task difficulty. We present ManiLadder, a large-scale and organized simulation benchmark for robotic manipulation. ManiLadder contains 112 carefully designed tasks stratified into 4 difficulty levels, with 8 categories in each level, spanning rigid-body and deformable-object manipulation, single-arm and dual-arm settings, and gripper and dexterous-hand end-effectors. To calibrate difficulty, we fix the data amount the same for every task and use a unified flow- matching imitation learning policy under the same training and evaluation protocols, then use its evaluation success rate as an operational empirical difficulty signal. All tasks are iteratively refined until they fall into target difficulty ranges, which are then verified across different random seeds, policies, and agentic learning systems. We further analyze the task properties, compare different policy performances and training settings, analyze task transfer and data scaling behaviors, and conduct real-world experiments showing that the designed tasks preserve their difficulty ordering beyond simulation. ManiLad- der provides a calibrated ladder for measuring, comparing, and advancing robotic manipulation capabilities. More results are available at https://maniladder-benchmark.github.io/.

Abstract: In long-horizon manipulation, a robot may need to take different actions under identical observations, depending on task progress and the outcomes of earlier attempts. We refer to this ambiguity as decision aliasing. Resolving it requires the policy to maintain memory of its execution history. To reduce the cost of processing full observation histories, recent methods build compact event memories using perceptual, semantic, or kinematic cues. However, these criteria may miss events that matter for task success. We instead propose an outcome-grounded principle: remember events that substantially change the predicted probability of eventual success. Following this principle, we introduce CGEM, a critic-gated event memory that uses a critic to select events with large estimated changes in success probability. Positive and negative changes are encoded as progress and failure records, respectively. Failure records are discarded once the corresponding subtask is completed. In a ten-subtask diagnostic environment with exactly computed success probabilities, CGEM achieves 88.0% success, compared with 0.0% for both a 36-step observation window and a kinematic event memory. Removing failure records reduces success to 1.7%.

</details>

Abstract: Vision-language models (VLMs) have become a powerful foundation for robotic scene understanding, task planning, and language-conditioned control. A natural question follows: can these models also decode what a human operator intends while teleoperating a robot, without any explicit instruction? We formulate this as hierarchical intent prediction, jointly inferring the ongoing atomic action and the broader mission it serves from a short visual teleoperation history. To study this, we introduce VI AMO (Vision Intention Action and Mission Ongoing), a dataset of 6,600 episodes from eleven robot sources with 13,653 temporally aligned action annotations and 9,702 mission annotations. Large proprietary models struggle with this problem: on held-out episodes, GPT-4o reaches Sentence-BERT similarities of only 0.563 and 0.470 for action and mission, and GPT-5.6 Luna achieves only 0.581 and 0.554. Fine-tuning a VLM on VI AMO yields our Vision-Intention Model (VIM), which raises these scores to 0.825 and 0.821 and still reaches 0.726 and 0.717 on the 35.4% of test actions with both labels absent from training. Finally, as a downstream application, VIM's predicted mission enables a vision-language-action (VLA) policy to take over from the operator mid-task in a real-world environment unseen during VIM fine-tuning, reducing human teleoperation time by 34.6% relative to GPT-4o and 39.8% relative to GPT-5.6 Luna, while cutting intent-prediction latency by roughly 70% on average.

Abstract: Whole-body motion tracking lets a humanoid robot follow reference motions, but training a tracking policy for a library of motions has usually taken hours of GPU time. SuperMimic showed that the policy can instead be trained by backpropagating through a world model learned from parallel simulation, and InstantTrack added Muon updates, a residual policy around the reference and an averaged deployed policy. We build on both and present BlinkMimic, a recipe that makes this training faster. On a simulated Unitree G1, BlinkMimic trains one policy for all 40 LAFAN1 clips in 3.4 minutes on a single RTX 4090, measured until three consecutive checkpoints reach a normalized return of at least 0.82 with no failed clip (mean of three seeds). We split training time into the cost of an iteration, the iterations needed to fit the motions, and the iterations needed to remove the last failures, and assign each component, inherited or new, to one of these terms. The new components are a step size that decreases once training has progressed, resets directed by failures of the deployed policy under deployment conditions, and GPU graph capture of the whole iteration. A leave-one-out ablation with three seeds per component shows which term each component affects, and further measurements of the gradient, the update rule and the failure cases explain these effects. Code and per-seed results are available at https://github.com/fuyichen0725/blinkmimic.

Abstract: Vision-based dexterous in-hand manipulation requires knowing not only where an object is, but also how it moves. However, most robotic manipulation systems rely on off-the-shelf visual perception models whose outputs are limited to static object masks, geometry, or 6D poses, leaving downstream controllers to infer object dynamics implicitly from noisy visual observations. We present VOKE, a kinematics-aware perception framework that augments existing object and hand-object pose estimators to recover a complete object kinematic state comprising pose, linear and angular velocity, and linear and angular acceleration. VOKE contains two core perception modules and a downstream application study. First, a Pluggable Kinematics Module (PKM) attaches to the output of a standard pose estimator and predicts the object's linear and angular velocity and acceleration from temporal visual features. Second, a Kinematics-guided Pose Refinement Module (KPRM) converts this motion vector into a second-order, motion-prior reference pose and refines the per-frame pose estimates. We evaluate VOKE on DexYCB and OakInk, where it consistently improves object pose estimation across three different backbones and substantially reduces velocity and acceleration estimation error compared with pose differencing. As a downstream application, we use Kinematics-Augmented Reinforcement Learning (KARL) to compare a kinematics-augmented policy with a pose-history baseline. After training the policies in simulation, we transfer the kinematics-augmented policy to the physical robot without policy fine-tuning, demonstrating the complete perception-to-control pathway in the target domain.

</details>

Abstract: Learning whole-body mobile manipulation requires demonstrations that coordinate bimanual actions with locomotion across extended workspaces. However, existing interfaces trade off direct robot control, perception and action alignment, and natural operator mobility, making such demonstrations difficult to collect. We introduce HALO, a human-aligned data collection system for whole-body mobile manipulation. The system aligns the operator's perception and action space with the robot while preserving direct physical feedback and natural locomotion. Operators guide the robot end-effectors through kinesthetic teaching and move a low-resistance platform without the deployed mobile base while performing bimanual manipulation. This interface enables more natural whole-body demonstrations, but the close human-robot coupling introduces discrepancies between the demonstration and deployment settings. We address these discrepancies with deployment-aligned demonstration processing, which calibrates and temporally aligns platform motion with the deployed base response and masks and inpaints demonstrator hands in recorded observations. Compared with Mobile ALOHA and VR teleoperation, HALO enables up to 2.9x faster demonstration collection while maintaining comparable or higher success rates. Abstract: Micro aerial vehicles that operate without GPS often run SLAM on ARM-based edge boards, yet there is little published data on how standard RGB-D SLAM systems behave on such hardware. RTAB-Map was configured to process and keep every frame, so its back end runs on every frame rather than at its default rate of 1 Hz. On the sequences that every configuration tracked, its front end takes 38-74 ms per frame and its full pipeline 150-462 ms, compared with 13-45 ms and 26-62 ms for ORB-SLAM3. RTAB-Map's main cost is optimizing a pose graph that keeps growing, because its working memory is unbounded by default; ORB-SLAM3's local bundle adjustment costs about as much as its tracking but runs in a parallel thread. At quarter resolution, both systems lose tracking on most sequences, and runs that lose tracking finish sooner and report lower error on the poses they keep, so time and accuracy must be read alongside coverage. Timing is repeatable to within 1-5%, but trajectory error varies by up to 27% between identical runs, so the accuracy differences between the two systems are inconclusive. No thermal throttling occurred.

Abstract: Low-impedance grippers make the dynamics of a grasp matter. Current grasp metrics, however, are static: they describe how a held object resists constant loads, not how it responds to time-varying ones. To quantify this, we rely on small-signal analysis to characterize a grasp by its admittance, the transfer matrix relating load perturbations to object displacements around an equilibrium. This transfer matrix recovers the stiffness and stability of the grasp at zero frequency and captures its natural frequencies and peak gain under time-varying loads. We apply this characterization to a planar two-finger gripper under proportional-derivative control. We show that two grasps of the same object in the same position and with the same contact forces can differ in stiffness by two orders of magnitude. Likewise, two grasps that share the same stiffness can exhibit very different resonances. Nonlinear simulations confirm the effectiveness of our small-signal analysis in capturing the dynamic behavior of grasping.

</details>

Abstract: Robotic manipulation often requires both spatial scene understanding and task history. However, many vision-language-action (VLA) policies rely primarily on current observations, without explicitly integrating spatial structure and task history. Appearance-based memories can be sensitive to occlusion and viewpoint changes, while spatial representations of the current scene alone do not preserve how the scene has changed during task execution. We introduce STVLA, a framework that incorporates features from a pretrained visual geometry foundation model into temporal memory alongside visual representations. This allows the policy to recall past scene structure as context for current action decisions. To select informative history, STVLA uses a mathematically grounded scoring rule that balances relevance to the current observation with complementary evidence from memory. Across evaluations on LIBERO, LIBERO-Mem, MIKASA-Robo, and SimplerEnv Bridge, STVLA achieves the highest average success rate among the compared methods on each benchmark. Abstract: Actions in expert demonstrations exhibit temporal structure. An ordered-versus-shuffled analysis across five environments confirms this dependence. We ask whether action proposals that better reflect expert continuity can improve visual world-model planning. Rather than imposing generic smoothness, we learn continuations from the temporal structure of demonstrated actions. Our locality-gated deep-kernel Gaussian-process prior, LG-DKGP, combines recent and longer-range executed action history, predicts a neural continuation, and learns a temporal Gaussian-process (GP) residual. A training-derived exploration scale initializes search around the corrected mean; a frozen world model evaluates progress toward the goal. Training first fits the neural mean, then jointly optimizes the prior with the GP residual using expert future actions. The resulting planner achieves 87.07\% macro success across five simulated tasks spanning native and fragment-level goal attainment, compared with 71.03\% for INTACT, 61.66\% for PRISM, and 58.66\% for LeWM. Component interventions and expert-relative action-structure comparisons support demonstration-informed continuations and reveal task-dependent effects of visual conditioning, history length, and planning horizon.

Abstract: Planning for a heterogeneous robot team from a scene image and an instruction involves two kinds of decisions. Some must be judged from the scene: which goals are already met, how the task splits, and which robot does what. Others follow from these and can be computed: prerequisite actions, handoffs between robots, and timing. We argue that each kind should go to the component suited to it: a small vision-language model learns the first, and a deterministic solver computes the second. In NSDL (Neuro-Symbolic Division of Labor), the trained model writes its judgments as a plan sketch, which lists the goals that still need action and the intended steps in order, each with a robot. The solver expands the sketch into an executable joint plan. The model calls two tools, a detector for evidence and the solver, and is trained with the solver in the loop. We evaluate on the public VIKI-L2 benchmark and on LOCUS-Bench, a new benchmark that grades the two kinds of difficulty separately. NSDL outperforms end-to-end planners and formalize-then-plan baselines on both benchmarks and with three backbones from 3B to 8B; the 7B model reaches 97.0% out-of-distribution accuracy on VIKI-L2. Ordering and scheduling errors nearly disappear once the solver writes the structure; the remaining failures are misjudged states, and most goals the model misses are ones it never lists. Code, model weights, and LOCUS-Bench are publicly available at https://github.com/Xincheng-Tan/NSDL.

</details>

A within-subjects user study with 12 participants across 252 trials demonstrates significant improvements in perceived smoothness (p = 0.003) and predictability (p = 0.034) over both a LiDAR-only baseline and a trajectory-prediction-only method, without any increase in travel time. Notably, trajectory prediction alone does not improve subjective navigation quality unless coupled with attention-state-aware cost adjustment -- confirming that appropriately responding to who is in front of the robot can matter more than trajectory smoothness alone.

A training-only embodiment-dreaming objective predicts latent representations of future visual and proprioceptive states, encouraging the shared trunk to model each embodiment's dynamics. Across 13 task-embodiment pairs spanning five tasks and six robot embodiments, including five physical robots and one simulated humanoid, cross-embodiment pretraining raises MXT+'s average normalized task progress from 0.63 to 0.80 over training from scratch.
