Abstract: Recent advances in frontier models have pushed the boundaries of agentic robot control, enabling agents to perform complex robotic tasks through reasoning and interaction. However, repeated calls to these models during execution can substantially increase task completion time. Code as Policies (CaP) offers a route for coding agents to acquire physical skills through experience and retain them in executable programs, allowing the reasoning invested in development to be reused across runs. Developing reliable code policies while limiting the cost of physical experimentation remains a central challenge. We investigate human guidance as a source of priors for feasible actions and simulation as an auxiliary environment for exploring and testing candidate policies. Our findings favor robot demonstrations available on request and simulation used selectively during real-world policy refinement. These findings inform ACE-CaP-0, an agentic recipe that couples policy revision with experience acquisition through Demonstrations on Request and Simulation on Demand. Across eight real-world manipulation tasks spanning alignment, multi-object handling, and bimanual coordination, the resulting frozen programs achieve 92.1\% success (221/240 trials). Mean execution times across tasks range from 0.97 to 2.27 minutes. The programs execute with runtime perception and control, without a human or coding agent in the loop. Abstract: Accurate environmental monitoring relies on Informative Path Planning (IPP) to deploy autonomous systems that maximize information gain. While Gaussian Processes (GPs) provide a robust mathematical foundation for modeling the environment, contemporary IPP approaches remain hindered by heuristic operational constraints and the lack of simultaneous theoretical guarantees for worst-case estimation error and approximation quality. Rather than introducing yet another heuristic, this paper presents a rigorous theoretical analysis of existing error bounds and algorithms to further develop the mathematical foundation for IPP.
  By evaluating probabilistic and deterministic bounds on reconstruction error, we establish that predictive variance is the theoretically ideal optimization objective for GP-based IPP. Furthermore, we analyze GreedyCover, a recent approach for optimizing predictive variance, and demonstrate that its near-optimal approximation guarantee matches the fundamental hardness-of-approximation limit for the problem. Comprehensive benchmarking confirms that this approach delivers superior estimation accuracy and computational efficiency compared to prominent information-theoretic objectives and optimization methods used in IPP. Finally, this theoretical analysis establishes a rigorous, near-optimal foundation for discrete offline IPP under bounded uncertainty constraints, offering clear and principled guidance for future work in continuous-space and online formulations.

</details>

Abstract: State-of-the-art LiDAR-inertial odometry methods, including those on richer state manifolds, achieve strong global accuracy, yet their pose stream alternates between propagation and correction and can oscillate over short horizons even when globally accurate. Most existing methods follow these approaches: they either modify the filter to enable faster updates or estimate a continuous-time trajectory from raw LiDAR and inertial data. The latter involves extra optimization, delaying state updates and becoming less robust during rapid rotations or accelerations. We propose CT-SGAL-LIO, a continuous-time refinement module that smooths the posterior pose stream of a discrete SGal(3) estimator without modifying the estimator itself. The key novelty is to perform continuous-time trajectory refinement directly on the projected SGal(3) posterior: we fit a cubic trajectory over a bounded window of recent poses and evaluate it at a small fixed lag. This places the query time within the observed interval, so the refined pose is obtained by interpolation rather than extrapolation, while preserving causal operation. CT-SGAL-LIO improves relative trajectory accuracy on 17 sequences from three public benchmarks. Across 17 sequences from GRAND-TOUR, MCD, and GEODE, CT-SGAL-LIO improves short-term motion accuracy while largely preserving global accuracy. RTE improves by up to 70% on GRAND-TOUR and on all seven GEODE sequences, with 9.9-51.1% reductions. A 19.5-min R-Campus run also reduces posterior jumps by 85.4% with only a 4.7 mm change in the end-to-start gap. We plan to release our source code and results to support community validation and reproducibility.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) can supply autonomous systems with plans, predictions, and judgments, but these contributions are sometimes wrong. This review asks how a system can maximize an LLM's utility while managing its fallibility. Our answer is to adapt the system's reliance on the LLM as evidence about the LLM's performance accumulates. Systems built from imperfect components, such as noisy sensors and faulty software, have long worked in this way, and the classical methods they use can be applied to LLM-based systems as well. A system can change its reliance on an LLM in several ways, depending on the LLM's role and on the other components in the system. Each change needs an estimate of the LLM's reliability, and the LLM's role and the kind of contribution it makes determine what to estimate. Classical methods from decision theory, estimation, and online learning then turn these estimates into reliance decisions. These decisions can reduce how often LLM errors affect the system. However, some errors matter even when they are rare. An independently verified mechanism can prevent such errors from being executed. Without one, adapting reliance using reliability estimates can still keep their risk within a bound. Studies on adapting reliance on LLMs are emerging but limited, and the reviewed comparisons suggest that adapting reliance can improve system performance.

</details>

Abstract: Embodied foundation models hinge on diverse robot interaction data, which are costly to collect. Egocentric human videos offer a complementary source of experience, and latent action models (LAMs) provide a popular means of leveraging these videos for policy supervision. Compared with world-action models (WAMs), LAMs can offer greater training efficiency and potentially lower inference costs by avoiding explicit pixel prediction during policy learning and execution. However, most existing LAMs learn latent actions solely through video reconstruction, which can cause their representations to cluster by visual appearance rather than by the underlying actions. As a result, they struggle to align human actions with their robot counterparts. To bridge the gap, we introduce SALA, which emphasizes establishing the semantic correspondence for latent actions. Without requiring paired data, we use the language descriptions of unpaired human and robot clips to estimate semantic similarities and supervise cross-embodiment correspondence. Specifically, we translate these similarities into soft neighborhood structures that guide human latent actions toward semantically related robot latents, while robot action reconstruction grounds the latent space in control. The resulting shared latent action space provides a unified supervision signal for training an embodied foundation model on both human and robot videos. Notably, latent action supervision enables scaling along two axes: (1) pre-training on increasing amounts of general human video consistently reduces robot action prediction error on downstream tasks, and (2) post-training on increasing amounts of task-specific human video substantially improves real-world robot task success rates.

</details>

Abstract: Humanoid loco-manipulation requires coordinated locomotion, balance regulation, and manipulation under frequent contacts, making teleoperated demonstration collection difficult: a practical controller must simultaneously provide precision, stability, and contact awareness. We propose FORGE, a force-aware multi-granularity control system with a coarse-to-fine command space spanning velocity navigation, planar foot placement, wrist-pose tracking, and optional force-conditioning inputs. Policy training requires no full-body human motion trajectories or joint-angle references; deployment consumes only sparse bilateral wrist poses relative to the pelvis, coarse motion commands, and optional desired wrist-load inputs. For sustained contact, FORGE combines a momentum observer with a learned correction trained using simulator contact-force supervision. At deployment, it estimates low-frequency, wrist-equivalent interaction forces from proprioception and reconstructed low-level arm PD torques, without wrist force/torque sensors. A quasi-static virtual-target loop uses this estimate and an optional desired applied-force input to adapt the wrist target. A frozen locomotion prior and asymmetric residual actions stabilize manipulation-induced whole-body corrections. Experiments evaluate unified-control stability, task-space tracking, quasi-static wrist-equivalent force estimation, squeezing forces during sustained contact with a rigid box, and downstream diffusion-policy execution of a squat-and-lift task across payload conditions. Abstract: Robots that learn from demonstrations should extrapolate their policy despite state perturbations such as sensor noise, scene variation, and compounding errors. One approach to this robustness is regularization. Prior regularizers consider each state on its own and limit how sensitive the policy is to any perturbation at that state. By contrast, we explore how the policy's output shifts when given the same perturbation at different states. Our insight is that, locally, the robot should respond to state perturbations in a consistent manner: e.g., if changing an irrelevant object position does not impact the policy at one state, that same change should not shift the policy output at a nearby state. Applying this insight, we develop inner product regularization (InneR). We show that the inner product on action space induces an inner product on state space through the policy Jacobian (the Fisher information for stochastic policies), and that the null space of this inner product governs which state perturbations have a first-order effect on the policy. By clustering states whose inner products are aligned, averaging the inner products within each cluster, and minimizing the smallest eigenvalues of that average, InneR shapes the learned policy so that nearby states share a common null space. InneR enforces consistent responses to perturbations without limiting policy expressiveness, and can be appended to existing imitation learning pipelines for deterministic or stochastic policies. In simulated experiments, InneR makes learned policies more robust to irrelevant state dimensions and shifts between training and test distributions, whereas norm penalties often perform worse than behavior cloning. On a real robot arm performing three manipulation tasks, InneR raises the average success rate of a diffusion policy from 18% to 57% without additional data.

</details>

Abstract: Embodied interaction requires an agent to integrate multimodal perception with verbal and nonverbal behaviors to effectively communicate its intentions, including speech, body motion, and facial expressions. We present XBot-Omni, a Thinker--Talker--Actor architecture that extends the Omni paradigm to robotic modalities, including body motion and facial expressions, with ``X'' representing the extensibility to robotic-specific modalities. The Thinker processes text, images, audio, and video to formulate contextually appropriate responses, which are then realized by the Talker and Actor through speech and coordinated bodily expressions, respectively. In this report, we innovatively introduce the concept of ``Actor'' as a dedicated component for embodied agents: a coordinated, streaming module that leverages the native representations of the Thinker and Talker within an end-to-end pipeline, introducing only modest latency overhead. This unified architecture enables multimodal generation conditioned on omni-modal inputs directly to prevent information loss in staged designs. We comprehensively evaluate XBot-Omni across seven benchmarks, demonstrating strong multimodal understanding capabilities on both general-domain and robot-centric videos, as well as high-quality, natural, and well-coordinated motion generation. Furthermore, our results validate its capability to generate embodied multimodal responses directly from omni inputs, meeting the diverse requirements of embodied interaction. XBot-Omni enables robots not only to perceive the omni world but also to express themselves through speech and bodily behaviors, extending Omni models from conversational interaction to embodied communication in physical environments.

Abstract: Language-guided UAV navigation has largely focused on long-horizon route planning, leaving short-range, object-relative flight maneuvers underexplored. To address this gap, we formulate Flying-on-a-Word (Flow), a task requiring a UAV to convert concise natural-language instruction, egocentric visual observation, and current state dynamics into continuous flight actions. Flow encompasses both primitive motion commands and complex object-interactive behaviors, assessing motion intent comprehension and spatial context grounding. To enable policy learning and closed-loop evaluation, we introduce UAV-Flow, a comprehensive dataset comprising 30,692 expert-piloted real-world trajectories across eight distinct motion categories, alongside 10,109 simulation trajectories in the UAV-Flow-Sim environment equipped with task-specific completion criteria. Furthermore, we develop FlowVLA, which undergoes imitation pretraining on mixed real-sim demonstrations and online post-training via Tail-Aware GRPO (TGRPO). By leveraging group reward distributions, TGRPO effectively reinforces high-reward action sequences. Evaluated on 273 held-out UAV-Flow-Sim episodes, pretrained FlowVLA achieves a mean success rate of 75.58%, outperforming the strongest baseline by 6.72 percentage points, which TGRPO post-training further elevates to 93.16% with superior sample efficiency. Physical UAV experiments demonstrate robust execution of object-relative commands in both indoor and outdoor real-world scenes. Together, Flow establishes a novel benchmark and learning framework for language-conditioned flight maneuvers.

Abstract: Multi-robot systems face the challenge of efficiently allocating teams of heterogeneous robots to tasks. To address these challenges, we present an iterative clustering algorithm for collaborative task allocation in heterogeneous multi-robot systems. This approach partitions the computationally intractable global optimization problem into smaller, tractable subproblems by iteratively forming clusters of robots and tasks, then optimizing assignments within each cluster. By ensuring robots remain clustered with their currently assigned tasks, we guarantee monotonic improvement in overall utility with each iteration. We analyze the convergence of the algorithm and characterize how cluster size constraints determine which suboptimal assignments could trap the algorithm. In simulation, iterative clustering consistently outperforms simulated annealing, and a group-based auction in both computation time and solution quality, and outperforms a hedonic game approach in solution quality.

</details>

Abstract: Vision language navigation (VLN) is very challenging for Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs) since small errors in 3D motion prediction can lead to navigation errors and collisions. Unable to fathom execution outcomes in 3D environments, existing methods often struggle to predict accurate navigation waypoints from language instructions. To solve this, we introduce the Prototypical Visual Imagination framework, shortened as ProtoVis-Nav, which forces the model to imagine an outcome-associated local visual representation for the intended next UAV movement. Specifically, we predict feature prototypes and use their linear combination to estimate the visual feature of the target region. In this manner, the VLN model first imagines the local visual representation of the next intended location, and then uses predicted prototypes as context for generating the final action, promoting alignment between visual context and waypoint outputs. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of the ProtoVis-Nav framework and show substantial improvements over existing baselines.

Abstract: Arena-style rankings of language models are a widely used evaluation framework in online leaderboards, research papers, and model evaluation in production. These rankings aim to realistically assess model performance using pairwise comparisons of anonymous models on user prompts, aggregating votes via the Bradley-Terry model to produce the final ranking. However, we find that these leaderboards are highly vulnerable to several manipulation strategies that unscrupulous providers could use to boost a model's rank: strategic voting (individual votes that boost a model's rank, including votes on irrelevant models), strategic prompting (choosing prompts that favor a model), and strategic nomination (boosting the rank of a model by inserting irrelevant models). We demonstrate these effects on public leaderboard data, analyze the circumstances that cause these vulnerabilities, and describe approaches to safeguard against each attack. Notably, these issues hold for any preference-based evaluation using the Bradley-Terry model that allows open prompting, voting, or model submission. These manipulations boost the rank of nearly every tested model; each attack can boost a model by up to 15-20 places in a ranking and, combined, they can boost a model by up to 45 places.

Abstract: Agents that perform state estimation using pretrained perception models, e.g., Vision-Language Models (VLMs), face the fundamental challenge that predictions are uncertain. Still, most approaches determinize these predictions into a single best-guess state and make decisions as if it were ground truth. This is brittle because when the guess is wrong, the agent's decisions may be unsafe or infeasible. At the other extreme, maintaining a full probability distribution over symbolic states is often intractable. We propose a middle path, drawing on the underlying probabilities the perception model already produces (e.g., a VLM's output-layer token distributions) to construct a $θ$-confidence Minimal Belief Set (MBS$_θ$), a minimal subset of states whose cumulative probability meets a user-specified belief-mass threshold. The minimality of an MBS$_θ$ ensures that the agent considers only the most likely states, enhancing robustness in downstream decision-making while maintaining computational tractability. Using a novel three-layered belief system, we efficiently approximate individual state probabilities from per-feature predictions and task-level constraints. On this belief construction, we base our MBS$_θ$ construction, while maintaining only a lean factored representation of the belief. Our algorithm, Probability-Ranked Valid State Search (PRaVaSS), takes advantage of the unique structure of this belief system to construct an MBS$_θ$ with correctness guarantees and polynomial complexity in the unconstrained case. Using a VLM as the backbone of our state estimator, we validate our method's theoretical properties, scalability with problem size, and improvement of downstream decision-making when used in a task-planning pipeline.

</details>

Abstract: Deployment of retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) for Edge-AI is constrained by memory and bandwidth limitations. This paper presents an engineering integration and empirical evaluation of a multimodal RAG framework optimized for consumer-grade Turing hardware (e.g., NVIDIA Tesla T4). Our system-level analysis identifies a critical trade-off: while generative pre-trained transformer quantization (GPTQ) offers superior throughput (51.6 tok/s), it exhibits significant context instability at 2048+ tokens on Turing architectures. Conversely, NormalFloat 4-bit (NF4) maintains robust long-context performance with viable latency (81 ms/tok). By coupling NF4 with a decoupled Florence-2 vision integration, we achieve visual reasoning with only 430 MB VRAM overhead. Furthermore, empirical observations of prompt-induced search space pruning show that structured prompting reduces latency by 47% without altering computational intensity (1.88 GFLOPs/token). The integrated architecture maintains an 8.37 GB footprint, establishing a viable RAG pipeline for 12-16 GB GPUs. Finally, structured few-shot prompting effectively compensates for quantization-induced reasoning loss, restoring mathematical accuracy from 35.0% to 92.0% across 200 trials.

Abstract: Does multimodal training shape how a language model thinks, even when the input is text only? We test this with targeted causal ablations across 10 multimodal configurations from 6 model families, with matched text-only backbones and cross-family transfer controls. We contrast spatial-imagery tasks that invite mental scene construction with symbol-manipulation tasks solvable by symbolic rules alone. We identify a vision-language axis in hidden-state space and ablate its projection at inference. In Chameleon-7B, this ablation drops accuracy on BIG-Bench Hard (BBH) navigate by 27.6 pp while leaving boolean expressions intact (+5.6 pp), a 33.2 pp double dissociation. The effect is direction-specific and layer-localized, extends to mental-simulation tasks such as mental rotation, paper folding, and perspective taking, and is not explained by task difficulty, reasoning depth, or sequential update load. Together, these results reveal emergent vision-shaped thinking (EVT): text-only reasoning that causally recruits representations introduced or strengthened by vision-language training, without assuming an image-like internal format. The measured effect is spatially concentrated at 7-8B and broadens to non-spatial visual attribute binding in the tested 32B configuration. In dual-path unified models (BAGEL, Janus-Pro), the causal handle localizes to the generation pathway as a compact one-dimensional carrier. The understanding pathway also encodes spatial information in a higher-rank, probe-decodable form, but is not the causal handle. To separate decodability from causal use, we analyze concentration, alignment, and semantic location. These diagnostics explain why behavior, probing, and intervention can diverge. Multimodal training thus reshapes the geometry of text-only reasoning, not just how visual input is processed.

</details>

Achieved without memory, evolution, or extensions, these results establish EvoX as a competitive multi-model agent runtime and underscore the importance of harness-level operational control in realizing foundation-model capabilities.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning sharpens LLM reasoning, but parameter updates are expensive, forgetful, and opaque; training-free libraries swap gradients for a textual store but still use trajectory-level, correlation-based admission and flat structure. To address both, we propose HyperSkill, a Training-Free Omnimodal GRPO framework that keeps the backbone frozen and replaces gradient updates with auditable edits to a Hypergraph-Indexed Skill-Library, where skills are advantage-bearing nodes and compositions are hyperedges. It has three components: training-time distillation admits counterfactually checked skills and learns a protocol skill from failures; gated hypergraph retrieval injects advantage-ranked skills where a per-dataset gate admits them; and an execution protocol makes the frozen backbone commit to an answer. On 11 text, vision-language, and omnimodal benchmarks, HyperSkill is best or tied on every dataset and beats the best baseline by 13.0 average F1 points while keeping the backbone frozen. Our software and data are publicly available at https://github.com/LHRLAB/HyperSkill.

Abstract: In robotics and embodied AI, a core challenge for world models is adapting to sudden, persistent, yet initially unknown changes in robot dynamics during interaction. For instance, when a robotic arm grasps an object with an unknown payload, the robot dynamics change suddenly. If the world model fails to adapt, its prediction errors accumulate over the open-loop rollout and ultimately mislead the robot's actions. Existing world models, however, struggle to simultaneously satisfy three critical requirements for dynamics adaptation: responsiveness, reacting promptly to sudden changes to curb prediction error accumulation; stability, distinguishing persistent changes from transient disturbances to prevent incorrect updates; and persistence, retaining the adapted dynamics without new observations so that later predictions do not revert to the old dynamics. Inspired by predictive coding in the human brain with multi-timescale adaptation, we propose AFTER-WM, a world model with Adaptive Fast-slow Timescales for Error-driven Retention, whose prediction errors drive a fast correction that fades and a slow correction that persists, while dynamically adjusting the combination weights of pre-trained experts without altering them, to overcome the trade-offs among responsiveness, stability and persistence. On five simulated robot datasets and real UAV data, AFTER-WM has a lower multi-step mean error than all ten compared models and the highest mean return in a joint-failure control task. In simulation, its multistep mean error is 16%-34% below the strongest compared model's and is also the lowest 1-4 steps after a change and under transient disturbances alone, and its step-40 error is the lowest as well, reflecting responsiveness, stability and persistence. All code (method, baselines, experiments) and datasets will be released online.

</details>

Abstract: Discrete diffusion generation is emerging as a competitive alternative to autoregressive generation by denoising the entire token sequence through iterative refinement. Its global planning and progressive correction capabilities are particularly well suited to autonomous driving. However, transferring this generation paradigm from general-purpose settings to driving introduces substantial domain gaps in both training and inference. To unlock its potential, we propose a multi-stage optimization pipeline for standard vision-language model architectures, without auxiliary action experts or specialized mixture-of-experts Transformer, spanning post-training and inference. During supervised fine-tuning, we optimize the representation of planning inputs to suppress hallucinations in task responses. During reinforcement learning, we introduce metric-guided diversity filtering to identify stronger learning starting points and improve policy exploration. At inference time, we propose Dynamic Autonomous Driving Decoding (DAD), which adaptively adjusts decoding modes according to scene complexity. Based on this pipeline, we develop Masked Diffusion Drive (MD-Drive), improving its performance by 29.7\% over the unoptimized model. Experiments on the NAVSIM closed loop benchmark and the nuScenes and DriveCoT open loop datasets show that MD-Drive outperforms autoregressive VLM baselines with the same architecture, while achieving a more favorable balance between performance and computational cost than systems with different architectures through discrete diffusion training and inference.

Abstract: Titan is a compelling target for future deep space exploration, while its extreme Earth-Titan distance and complex environment make continuous ground-in-the-loop planning impractical. Existing planning methods, primarily developed for Earth-observation scenarios, have limited generalizability to uncertain deep space conditions and adaptability to time-varying mission states. To address these challenges, this paper proposes a physics-constrained maskable proximal policy optimization (PCM-PPO) method for Titan autonomous mission planning. A Titan-specific discrete-time Markov decision process is established to characterize mission feasibility and resource constraints. A system dynamics and geometric model is incorporated to enhance physical representation, including central gravity, J2 perturbation, third-body attraction, and downlink occultation. To accommodate opportunity targets under a bounded action dimension, PCM-PPO organizes predefined and opportunity targets through a fixed-capacity target pool and represents the mission state with a target-wise state matrix and a global state vector. A physics-constrained action mask constructs an admissible decision domain from current mission states, guiding policy learning toward physically executable decisions and avoiding infeasible exploration. An integrated training strategy facilitates policy optimization under coupled constraints. Comparative, ablation, and uncertainty experiments validate the effectiveness of PCM-PPO. PCM-PPO provides a feasible and interpretable scheme for autonomous mission planning of Titan deep space probes.

Abstract: Compositional diffusion planners extend short-horizon trajectory models to long-horizon goal-conditioned planning by generating and stitching multiple local trajectory chunks. However, in multimodal environments, local plausibility is not sufficient for global consistency: independently initialized chunks may denoise toward incompatible overlap modes, producing plans that are individually feasible but incoherent after stitching. Existing remedies typically add search, guidance, pruning, or graph-based structure at inference time, increasing computational cost or relying on task-specific machinery. We instead investigate graph-structured dependence among chunk-wise initial diffusion noises as a lightweight coordination mechanism for compositional trajectory planning. We propose \textbf{M}arginal-Preserving \textbf{C}oupled \textbf{D}iffusion \textbf{C}omposition (MCDC), a training-free inference method for pretrained compositional diffusion planners. Instead of sampling chunk noises independently, MCDC constructs coupled Gaussian initializations whose per-chunk marginals remain exactly standard Gaussian, as required by the pretrained denoiser, while neighboring chunks receive positively correlated noise components. This preserves the standard-Gaussian initialization marginal of each chunk while biasing adjacent chunks toward compatible overlap-mode choices. We prove exact marginal preservation and show, under a simple multimodal overlap model, that positive coupling increases the probability of compatible mode selection. On OGBench long-horizon stitch tasks, MCDC consistently improves over independent compositional diffusion sampling while retaining nearly the same inference time, and achieves a favorable success--cost trade-off compared with search-based composition methods.

</details>

Abstract: Enterprise agents accumulate memory across dozens of sessions in which facts contradict, get superseded, and blur as similarly named entities collide in embedding space. The field treats this as a retrieval problem; we show it is not. On ENT-310, a synthetic enterprise sales-agent memory benchmark of 310 queries (210 answerable, 100 unanswerable), a vanilla Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) reader over a memory store already matches strong memory systems on the recall pillars. Retention and currency are near-saturated, roughly 0.93 to 0.98 for every competent system, so recall is essentially solved. What remains unsolved is resolution: which entity a query refers to, which time-state is current, and whether to commit an answer at all or decline when evidence is insufficient. We propose DISCERN (DISambiguation and Cross-session Episodic memoRy Navigation), an agent-memory framework built on a single rule: preserve at write, resolve at read. At write time DISCERN builds a strictly additive bi-temporal provenance graph that never merges confusable entities and never drops superseded values. At read time a zero-LLM tool surface drives an agentic loop that navigates this graph and commits exactly one of four gated terminal actions. A four-judge LLM-as-a-Judge council scores two axes: answer correctness on answerable questions, and the answer-versus-abstain decision as a classification problem. On ENT-310, DISCERN is the best-calibrated system on abstention (F1 0.924 versus at most 0.802 across eight baselines) and leads macro answer correctness under equal pillar weighting (0.861 versus 0.806 for the strongest baseline), a lead driven by entity resolution (0.800, where no baseline exceeds 0.40). Applied with zero tuning to the full LoCoMo corpus, the same pipeline again leads macro answer correctness (0.663 versus 0.638) and stays among the best-calibrated on false-premise abstention.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous AI agents are increasingly used to automate biomedical research, creating a growing need to benchmark their capabilities on real scientific data and workflows. Their reliability in neuroimaging remains insufficiently characterized, where plausible analytical outputs can conceal errors that affect scientific conclusions. Existing studies have advanced scientific question answering and tool-based automation, but provide limited evidence on workflow reconstruction, acquisition of procedural expertise and evidence-based scientific judgment. Here we present NIBench, a neuroimaging benchmark comprising three complementary evaluations of autonomous agents. First, we benchmark execution and workflow reconstruction using 56 tasks across seven tool ecosystems, covering structural, diffusion and functional magnetic resonance imaging, positron emission tomography and multimodal analyses. Second, we examine whether agents can evolve their procedural skills through distillation of execution trajectories, evaluating the resulting skills on previously encountered tasks, unseen tasks and other models. Third, we assess scientific judgment using 36 claims from published Alzheimer's disease studies, requiring agents to analyse raw cohort data and provide reproducible evidence. We evaluate 19 language models within a shared agent framework, with selected models undergoing skill distillation and claim evaluation. Leading agents execute supplied analysis protocols nearly perfectly, whereas withholding procedural guidance widens performance differences. Distilled skills improve performance on acquisition tasks and transfer between models, but benefits on unseen tasks are inconsistent. Abstract: Embodied agents make decisions by combining information from heterogeneous memory modules, including declarative memory for explicit knowledge, procedural memory for learned routines, and working memory for current observations. During interaction, the memory contents required for decision making change with the agent's state, goal, and task progress, while many contents remain shared across consecutive decisions. However, existing memory modules are typically accessed through separate interfaces, so each updated memory context is often processed as a new input to the foundation model. This prevents selective key-value (KV) cache reuse for unchanged memory contents and forces full KV cache recomputation even when much of the memory context remains valid. We introduce HeMeR, an inference framework that performs KV reconciliation between dynamically updated memory contexts and reusable KV states. HeMeR tracks declarative, procedural, and working memory contents across action decisions, preserves KV states for unchanged contents, and reconciles only the KV states affected by memory-content updates. This enables efficient and stable inference as memory contents evolve during interaction. We evaluate HeMeR on ALFWorld, Habitat, and real-world robotic deployments requiring coordinated use of heterogeneous memory contents. In the real-world setting, HeMeR achieves 83.20% task success and reduces per-step inference time from 2.94 to 1.87 seconds compared with MemRL, while preserving compatible KV states as task progress updates the memory context.

</details>

Abstract: As agents perform tasks autonomously in local and cloud environments, auditing their execution trajectories can help service providers identify risks and improve agent harnesses and foundation models. Our analysis of production and red-team trajectories shows that some risks are repeatedly missed across auditing configurations. Overall recall obscures which risks are missed and whether higher recall comes with more false positives on legitimate behavior. Existing benchmarks offer limited insight into these failures. Generated trajectories can contain inconsistent tool calls and responses, conspicuous risky actions, or repetitive attack patterns. Grouping results by risk type also makes it difficult to tell whether auditors overlooked evidence, accepted an unsupported conclusion, or lacked information needed to make a judgment.
  We introduce ReA-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark for auditing responsible agent behavior. It organizes auditing challenges into six tiers: internal safety knowledge, contradictions within trajectories, evidence retrieval in long contexts, verification of sources and reasoning, indirect steering, and missing information. It includes challenging benign cases in which actions resemble risky ones but are consistent with the task and its authorization. It also provides paired trajectory versions to test whether auditors detect the same risks more reliably when the trajectory is shortened or missing information is supplied. Most samples are drawn from real production trajectories, with scenarios manually selected and adapted to preserve realistic tasks and risks that could plausibly arise during execution.

</details>

Abstract: Reinforcement learning can teach vision-language models (VLMs) to decline harmful image-text requests, but how safe the result becomes is set indirectly, through reward weights found by trial runs. Stating safety as budgets on the rate of harmful assistance and the rate of over-refusal, enforced by Lagrange multipliers inside GRPO with an off-the-shelf scorer as the cost, is the natural fix, and it fails twice. The multiplier compares the scorer's mean with the budget, and scorers built for ranking get the mean wrong: a fixed-point analysis shows that the mean bias, not the budget, then decides whether the multiplier settles, saturates or switches off. And standard GRPO standardizes the penalized reward within each group, which cancels the multiplier before it reaches the gradient. Two-channel constrained GRPO repairs both: the base reward keeps the raw score for the within-group comparison, the penalty and the constraint estimate pass through a monotone map fitted on judge-labeled training responses, and the advantage keeps the multiplier. On a Qwen3-VL-2B policy with a training-free readout of an open 4B model as the scorer, held-out harm on 1,037 adversarial prompts falls from 8.9% under standard GRPO to 4.8%, lower in every seed, where the uncalibrated budget gives 7.6%; over-refusal stays at most 2% on 400 benign queries and MMBench is unchanged. The gain is largest where the intent sits in the image (10.1% -> 5.1%) and grows on the unseen HADES benchmark (28.9% -> 12.8%). Order-preserving distortions of the scores reproduce each predicted controller state in every seed, and the results hold under a map fitted on another judge's labels, an image-reading judge and a human audit.

</details>

Abstract: Public health agencies need to anticipate how outbreaks will unfold, when they will peak, and how long demand on health services will remain elevated. We previously developed a foundation model, Mantis, which was trained entirely on simulated outbreaks---enabling us to generate the diversity and scale of training data needed to support forecasting across diseases. However, our previous work left unresolved when simulation improves predictions and how it should complement observational data.
  Here, we varied the balance of simulated and observed training data while holding forecasting tasks fixed and varying the available surveillance history. We found that simulation-rich training was particularly valuable with limited local history, and its relative advantage diminished as more observations became available. We also found that simulation-based training reduced exceptionally large forecast errors, suggesting that mechanistic knowledge can help compensate for limited local evidence.
  Building on these findings, we developed Mantis 2, which combines simulated and observational training to jointly forecast up to six variables sixteen weeks ahead. Across 30 forecasting tasks, Mantis 2 achieved the best aggregate probabilistic accuracy over short, medium, and long horizons among the machine learning, statistical, and baseline models evaluated. It missed roughly 30\% fewer outbreak peaks than the strongest comparison model, with successfully anticipated peaks identified more than six weeks in advance on average. We publicly release Mantis 2, enabling public health agencies and researchers to forecast disease outcomes without developing a separate model for each disease or location.

The system distills local adjustments from user requirements and execution feedback and stores them as Adapters linked to general-purpose skills. The routing agent selects general-purpose skills and applicable Adapters based on the current subtask, execution progress, and environmental feedback, incrementally maintaining a task-level online skill library that the main LLM reads on demand. The system preloads candidates for subsequent nodes and rechecks their applicability once actual inputs are determined. Reinforcement learning optimizes the two types of decisions using modification gains and routing outcomes, respectively.
  We evaluate Skill Adapter on ALFWorld, WebShop, and SkillsBench. Abstract: Electroencephalography (EEG) foundation models are pretrained on thousands of hours of recordings, yet their frozen features transfer poorly to downstream tasks, and this transfer does not consistently improve with larger models or more pretraining data. We show that a vision foundation model that has never seen EEG is a stronger frozen EEG encoder than these specialized models, once the signals are rendered as images that a vision transformer can read. Our method, SeeEEG, keeps the vision backbone frozen and learns only how EEG is displayed to it. Each channel occupies a dedicated row of patches and is drawn either as a waveform at a fixed gain or as a scalogram whose frequency range spans the patch height, so that task-relevant patterns fall within individual tokens. The montage, filtering, and contrast parameters of this display are initialized from a standard clinical montage and optimized with the task loss through the frozen backbone, which amounts to at most 1,113 trainable parameters per view. We compare SeeEEG with seven EEG foundation models on eight clinical, sleep, affective, and brain-computer interface datasets, with every encoder frozen and paired with identical readout heads. With a frozen DINOv3 ViT-B, SeeEEG outperforms every EEG foundation model in balanced accuracy on all eight datasets and reaches a mean rank of 1.3 in Cohen kappa or AUROC, compared with 2.9 for the strongest baseline, REVE. The learned displays transfer without further optimization to the video encoder V-JEPA 2.1, which outperforms every EEG foundation model on both metrics on six of the eight datasets. Replacing DINOv3 with a randomly initialized ViT-B lowers the score by 15.2 points on average, so the ability to read rendered EEG comes from visual pretraining.

FedNova showed the most balanced classification performance between PD and WMD. L1-norm structural pruning at 20%, 30%, and 40% sparsity was applied to reduce model transmission size. FedAvg, FedProx, and FedBN showed a tendency of decreasing Test AUC under pruning, while FedNova achieved improved Test AUC across all sparsity levels. Abstract: StructRL turns the experience collected during reinforcement learning into an explicit replay graph. Once the graph contains a path to the task goal, dynamic programming selects actions on the observed part of the state space; these actions form a graph policy that is defined only where the required transitions have been observed. StructRL transfers these actions to model-free learners: as action preferences for a DQN Q-model and as target directions along shortest observed paths for a SAC actor. For DQN on three sparse-reward MiniGrid tasks, final success rises from 0.10 to 0.90 (DoorKey), 0.10 to 0.80 (KeyCorridor) and 0 to 0.50 (ObstructedMaze); plain DQN does not catch up with three times the budget. For SAC on continuous PointMaze, graph-derived direction labels raise final success from 0.17 to 1.0 on seven matched runs that reached the goal. Distilling the graph policy into a fresh Q-model gives a DoorKey 6x6 controller that reaches the goal from 91% of reachable states, against 38% for plain DQN. Scaling experiments identify three bottlenecks: incomplete graph support, structural aliasing, and loss of graph information in high-dimensional inputs.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) is now a standard stage of language model post-training, and the compute devoted to it is growing rapidly. While it has been shown to improve capabilities, particularly for tasks with verifiable rewards, it can also change behaviours and internal properties that the reward does not explicitly target. We present a formalism to describe the evolution of "traits" during RL post-training and, in doing so, recover the Price equation from evolutionary biology. We use this to develop a mathematical model for what we call "trait hitchhiking": the amplification or suppression of non-targeted traits as training redistributes probability across completions. We evaluate this Price update expression on language models trained with GRPO and show that the Price estimates closely track the sampled trait dynamics. These results identify trait hitchhiking as a mechanism by which RL post-training can silently amplify undesirable traits.

Abstract: Tables are compositional, with content spread across a schema, cell values, rows, and columns. Large-scale operations such as retrieval, overlap estimation, deduplication, and data curation need these local signals composed into one reusable embedding vector per table. Table representation methods, however, are mostly evaluated through row-level prediction or supervised downstream tasks, which do not measure table-level embedding quality directly. We introduce a benchmark that evaluates table-level embeddings through four desiderata: consistency under partial views, discriminability across label granularities, robustness to benign perturbations, and efficiency. Across twelve methods on synthetic and real corpora, lightweight schema- and text-based fingerprints are among the strongest. The sharpest contrast comes from two tabular foundation models, TabPFN and ConTextTab: although both are strong row-level predictors, their states, composed by mean, max, or three further parameter-free pooling rules, trail the strongest fingerprints. We therefore ask whether the gap lies in the encoder or in the compositional rule. Keeping ConTextTab frozen, we learn only the compositional rule over its cell states, using support-conditioned distribution matching (SCDM), a self-supervised loss we propose that matches the distribution of embedding similarities to the distribution of partition overlaps within and across tables. Across all fifteen head configurations, the learned rule raises the Spearman correlation between embedding similarity and partial-view overlap from 0.04 to 0.43-0.50 on synthetic data and improves triplet ranking, clustering, and retrieval, with smaller but systematic gains on real data. A downstream parent-table retrieval task confirms these findings. How local states are composed, and with what objective, substantially affects the quality of table-level embeddings.

</details>

Abstract: Autonomous cyber defense is widely studied with deep reinforcement learning, but most work trains policies inside simulators because learning requires many trial-and-error interactions. Simulator-based training carries a fidelity gap: trained policies face authentication delays, partial action failures, service dependencies, and user traffic patterns that the simulator did not represent, and degrade on transfer. Training directly in the network being defended removes the gap but makes every interaction slow and costly, so sample efficiency becomes the binding constraint. Model-based reinforcement learning has reduced sample requirements on games and physical robots by orders of magnitude by planning inside a learned world model, but it has not been applied to defenders trained in operational networks. We present FOE-Dreamer (Factored Opponent-Enhanced Dreamer), a model-based reinforcement learning framework that trains a defender online and directly in a network on cloud infrastructure, without a simulator stage. FOE-Dreamer separates three sources of variation in the latent state: a controllable component driven by defender actions, an exogenous component driven by benign user activity, and an opponent embedding inferred from defender-observable signals alone. Separating the components reduces the variance in policy gradients during planning in imagination. Against two scripted attacker profiles with user activity emulated by the GHOSTS framework on an 8-host OpenStack testbed, FOE-Dreamer trains within a 3-day budget on a single GPU and achieves about 2$\times$ better episode loss than Rainbow and IQN baselines under matched compute. Both the factoring and the opponent model contribute to the gain, and the learned latents are consistent with the intended separation.

</details>

Abstract: Safe trajectory tracking (STT) aims to enable autonomous vehicles to follow a reference trajectory accurately while avoiding collisions. This problem is particularly challenging for Connected and Autonomous Vehicles (CAVs) in highly dynamic environments. Existing non-learning-based STT methods often use simplified vehicle and interaction models to meet real-time requirements, which may result in suboptimal tracking accuracy. Although Deep Reinforcement Learning (DRL) can help address this limitation, widely used Lagrangian DRL methods often suffer from unstable training because increasing Lagrange multipliers amplify policy-gradient magnitudes. To address these issues, we propose a novel reinforcement learning framework for STT, called STT Control Epigraph Form RL (STTC-EFRL). In this framework, the STT problem is formulated as an epigraph-form min-max optimization problem. Based on this formulation, we develop a learning mechanism that uses a decreasing scalar related to the epigraph to adjust the policy gradient descent direction without increasing the gradient magnitude, thereby improving training stability. We also develop a computationally efficient ray-casting method to compute a polyhedron scaling factor for safety quantification. Experimental results, including Vehicle-in-the-Loop tests, show that STTC-EFRL achieves higher tracking accuracy than the considered non-learning-based baseline and more stable training than widely used Lagrangian DRL methods, while satisfying safety requirements in connected environments.

</details>

Abstract: Large language models trained with Reinforcement Learning (RL) with verifiable rewards exhibit strong reasoning ability and broad generalization, whereas models trained with Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) are often viewed as more prone to memorization and limited transfer. This paper rethinks this distinction through the lens of SFT training data on widely used Qwen2.5 and Qwen3 series models. First, we study the role of data source and show that it is critical: a carefully mixed SFT dataset substantially outperforms data generated solely by a larger model. Second, we study the role of data scale and show that matching the number of correct rollouts between SFT and RL greatly improves SFT generalization, while matching the total rollout budget enables SFT to generalize as well as RL. Combining these two factors further enables SFT to generalize even better than RL. Third, by using LLM annotations to characterize the solution methods in training rollouts, we show that larger datasets cover more tail methods and that these tail methods provide generalizable reasoning signals. Finally, we support these empirical findings theoretically by analyzing the training dynamics of shallow transformers under both RL and SFT.

Abstract: A model that forecasts sales accurately can still mislead a retailer about how sales respond to a price change. We evaluate released tabular and time-series foundation models, as well as a foundation model pretrained specifically to estimate causal effects. We report three findings. First, forecast accuracy does not indicate the accuracy of the predicted effect of price, both on simulated data in which the true effect is known and on real retail sales records. Second, for the tabular foundation models, additional context improves forecasts but not the predicted effect of price. Third, instrumental variables, the standard remedy in econometrics, reduce this error only when supplied in a specific form and increase it for every model under confounding when supplied as raw instruments.

Abstract: Modern machine learning models for global weather forecasting achieve remarkable predictive accuracy, yet they frequently produce states that violate fundamental physical laws, including conservation of mass, hydrostatic balance, or spectral energy scaling. We term these outputs physical hallucinations and propose PhysXAI, a rigorous diagnostic framework that detects, localises, and attributes such inconsistencies. Unlike existing post-hoc metric suites, PhysXAI is a learnable system that explicitly decouples forecast error from physical plausibility. We formalise the problem through a novel joint condition on numerical and physical residuals, and introduce a multi-branch encoder that extracts representations from conservation, dynamical, spectral, cross-variable, and spatiotemporal residuals. A key innovation is a physics-consistency contrastive learning objective, which we theoretically show the latent representation to be approximately isometric to the physical residual space, ensuring that diagnostic decisions remain grounded in physically meaningful deviations. We further derive an adaptive multi-objective loss with gradient-based weighting to balance competing diagnostic objectives. The framework produces spatially resolved hallucination maps, violation-type classifications, and calibrated Bayesian uncertainty, enabling both detection and interpretable attribution of physical inconsistencies. We conduct a comprehensive comparative evaluation of PhysXAI across ERA5, WeatherBench 2, and MERRA-2, using forecasts from GraphCast, Pangu-Weather, and NeuralGCM, with Python-written code and a fully reproducible experimental protocol. Our theoretical analysis and empirical evaluation establish PhysXAI as a principled framework for diagnosing physical hallucinations and advancing trustworthy AI for weather forecasting.

</details>

Abstract: Many engineering design problems involve multiple conflicting objectives and strict feasibility constraints. They are typically solved with population-based optimizers that require many evaluations and offer limited online control over where the search is focused. We present MOON-RL, a reinforcement-learning-based optimization framework that formulates constrained multi-objective search as preference-directed navigation in the objective space. In MOON-RL, a lightweight PPO policy receives objective-space and constraint-related observations and acts as an instance-specific adaptive search process. It learns how to update the design so that the current solution advances along a user-specified trade-off direction, while a simple meta-algorithm periodically resets the policy, resamples starting designs from a continuously updated archive of non-dominated solutions, and adjusts step-size and penalty schedules. We evaluate MOON-RL on real-world actuator-design problems and additional convergence-hard constrained benchmarks, comparing against established evolutionary algorithms under matched evaluation budgets. In focused search scenarios, MOON-RL achieves competitive performance in the region of interest, and ablation studies show that both the RL-driven adaptive sampling process and the meta-algorithm contribute to this behaviour, suggesting that repurposing RL training as objective-space navigation is a viable building block for interactive, preference-guided design tools. Additional experiments show that the framework also accommodates adaptive non-RL samplers, while PPO remains a strong choice for fixed-target, preference-directed objective-space navigation.

</details>

Abstract: The Flexible Job-Shop Scheduling Problem (FJSP) is a fundamental and challenging optimization problem in smart manufacturing. Although recent deep reinforcement learning (DRL) approaches can learn effective scheduling policies through interaction with the environment, their performance often degrades as problem scale and processing-time uncertainty increase. In this paper, we propose DredRL, a dual representation enhanced diffusion-policy reinforcement learning scheduler. DredRL first constructs enhanced dual representations of operations and machines through self- and cross-attention mechanisms to capture both intra-entity and inter-entity dependencies. A diffusion model is further employed as the policy network, generating scheduling decisions through iterative denoising conditioned on the encoded state to enhance policy expressiveness and robustness. Extensive experiments on synthetic, public, large-scale, and stochastic FJSP benchmarks demonstrate that DredRL outperforms state-of-the-art learning-based schedulers across most configurations. Compared with SOTA learning-based methods, DredRL reduces makespan by up to 5.44% on synthetic instances. It also achieves the best learning-based performance on three of four public benchmarks and outperforms the stochasticity-aware algorithms on stochastic benchmarks. These results demonstrate that combining enhanced dual representations with diffusion-based policies provides an effective and robust approach for large scale scalable FJSP scheduling in complicated environment.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning (RL) has become central to post-training large language models (LLMs). However, popular RL methods like GRPO incur non-negligible overhead by computing both old-policy and current-policy likelihoods to form importance sampling ratios. In this work, we propose Likelihood-Gated Policy Optimization (LGPO), which enforces a soft trust region constraint via likelihood-based gating, eliminating the need to compute old-policy likelihoods. Empirically, we show that removing the importance sampling correction term does not harm training stability, whereas removing the trust region mechanism can lead to collapse. Moreover, ratio-based clipping can fail in fully on-policy training: the importance ratio stays at 1, so the ratio-based trust region constraint never activates. Under standard training settings where GRPO is stable, LGPO achieves comparable training stability and peak performance while reducing training time by ~18% on average relative to a standard implementation that recomputes old-policy likelihoods. In fully on-policy training, where GRPO fails, LGPO remains stable, enabling more efficient and robust LLM RL post-training across training regimes.

Abstract: Recent architectural advances have improved time-series forecasting. However, our analysis of forecasters with diverse architectures reveals a common pattern: a substantial share of residual energy is concentrated in a small number of low-frequency components. Simple linear probes further show that the forecasters' historical inputs still contain predictive information about errors in the forecast mean. Building on these findings, we propose Model-Output Residual Adaptation (MORA), a model-agnostic residual adapter that refines the outputs of frozen forecasters without requiring additional inputs. MORA estimates segment-wise means of the target sequence through structural extrapolation and learned estimation, rather than directly predicting backbone-specific residuals. It then converts discrepancies between these estimates and the corresponding statistics of the backbone forecast into stagewise corrections, scaling each update according to estimation reliability. Extensive experiments on twelve benchmarks with six forecasting backbones show average MSE reductions of 7.2% for long-term forecasting and 28.1% for short-term forecasting, with lower MSE in 96.2% of the 288 evaluated cases. The benefits also extend to three time-series foundation models evaluated at different context lengths. Together, these results demonstrate that predictable coarse-scale residuals can be effectively exploited to improve existing forecasts. Code repository: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/MORA-B0B0/.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards has shifted the alignment paradigm of large language models from subjective preference to objective correctness, yet a fundamental disconnect persists between exploration diversity and reasoning validity. Existing strategies predominantly rely on "blind" entropy metrics, e.g., token-level or global sequence-level entropy, which structurally fail to distinguish between effective reasoning and potential hallucinations. This misalignment creates an illusion of diversity, where high entropy tends to signal chaotic degeneration rather than valuable exploration. To bridge this gap, we introduce Effective Entropy, a validity-aware entropy concept that quantifies diversity exclusively within the valid subspace and systematically filters out the noise of incorrect trajectories. Built on this concept, we instantiate VEPO, a simple Validity-aware Entropy Policy Optimization method that directly incorporates effective entropy into RLVR training. VEPO induces an adaptive attraction dynamic that drives up the probability of under-explored valid trajectories, a mechanism that matches the practical regime of large-scale autoregressive models where sequence probabilities remain extremely small. Experiments on six mathematical reasoning benchmarks with 1.5B and 7B DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen models show consistent aggregate accuracy gains over GRPO and competitive repeated-sampling success. A direct evaluation with matched correct counts finds more distinct strategies on commonly solved MATH500 problems, supported by paired statistical tests and three-seed training comparisons.

Abstract: Deep Reinforcement Learning (RL) plays a crucial role in tackling complex tasks, yet its reliance on deep neural networks makes the learned policies opaque. To enhance the trustworthiness of RL policies, existing work (e.g., the SILVER framework [Li et al., 2025]) has focused on Shapley-based interpretability, but is typically restricted to low-dimensional state spaces, binary-action settings, and a single class of linear surrogate. We propose ReShap, a policy-distillation framework that extends Shapley-based interpretability to high-dimensional, multi-action environments. ReShap extracts compact feature representations from raw image observations, performs SHAP-based feature attribution to characterize how each feature contributes to the policy's decisions, and distills the deep RL policy into an interpretable surrogate trained on the full set of (state, policy-action) pairs along the policy's visited distribution. We instantiate the surrogate with three interpretable model families (decision trees, linear regression, and logistic regression) and compare their fidelity and task return. We evaluate ReShap on Atari and Box2D environments using three deep RL algorithms and conduct a human-subject study to assess the clarity and trustworthiness of the resulting policies. Results show that ReShap preserves competitive task performance, that decision trees generally outperform linear and logistic regression, and that linear regression is weakest in most settings.

Abstract: Deep neural networks for electromagnetic signal processing are typically developed as single-task experts with fixed inputs and objectives, limiting cross-task transferability. Electromagnetic foundation models offer broader task coverage but incur substantial pretraining and inference costs. To balance accuracy and efficiency, we adopt model routing to dynamically dispatch unknown signals to matched experts within a heterogeneous pool. We construct HeteroSignal-7, a multi-task routing benchmark with 140,000 samples from seven datasets covering six task types, paired with seven classification and waveform-recovery experts and a unified evaluation protocol. We then propose GLCR, a two-stage routing method combining global dispatch with local discrimination of similar tasks. First, a lightweight one-dimensional convolutional neural network (CNN) with an additive-margin head predicts one of seven routing classes. Second, a gradient-boosted-tree specialist uses four physical statistics to re-evaluate samples whose top two candidates match a confusion pair automatically identified from validation errors. The pipeline is trained across the full signal-to-noise ratio (SNR) range and requires neither SNR labels nor noise estimation at inference time. GLCR outperforms all 19 baselines, achieving 96.16% routing accuracy and a 76.30% system score, exceeding the respective second-best baselines by 1.16% and 1.14%. This work provides a public evaluation platform and an efficient, reproducible baseline for heterogeneous expert routing in electromagnetic signal processing.

Abstract: Foundation models are increasingly used across the sciences, but whether their internal computations correspond to known scientific operations or proceed through different mechanisms remains unclear. Understanding these mechanisms is essential for assessing reliability, identifying failure modes, and learning new science from the models. We present, to our knowledge, the first causal mechanistic interpretability study of an astronomy foundation model, AION-1, focusing on its multimodal advantage in redshift prediction, where combining imaging with photometry outperforms photometry alone. We find that this advantage is largest for spatially extended and dust-attenuated galaxies, and that most of it is localized to the first of the encoder's twelve blocks. Within that block, how well a property is linearly decodable does not predict whether it can be localized. Spatial extent is causally used, and one attention head dominates linear probing for it, yet ablating that head has negligible effect on redshift prediction: the information is distributed across heads and is decodable again one layer after we erase it. Dust attenuation shows the opposite pattern: it is only weakly decodable anywhere in the encoder, yet a single head is necessary for most of the multimodal advantage, particularly for the most dust-attenuated galaxies, and keeping that head as the only active head in its block preserves nearly all of the advantage. None of the astrophysically motivated operations we tested explains what this head computes. Whether this reflects a limitation of current interpretability methods or a computation that does not correspond to any known astrophysical operation remains open, and resolving it is a prerequisite for using interpretability as a tool for scientific discovery.

</details>

Abstract: Despite impressive advances in large language models (LLM), vision-language models (VLM) still exhibit limited spatial reasoning capabilities. Recent progress in feed-forward 3D reconstruction has enabled precise, real-time geometric understanding from visual inputs. In this work, we propose SpaceMCP, a framework that leverages these advances to introduce an MCP-style paradigm for spatial reasoning. Our approach augments a VLM with structured function calls, allowing it to query a scene graph for spatial information such as distances, object positioning, and camera properties. The scene graph is constructed in real time using feed-forward 3D reconstruction to recover metric geometry, combined with open-vocabulary segmentation to identify and localize relevant objects within the scene. Paired with the VLM's strong reasoning capabilities, the VLM is able to combine this primitive information to solve more complex spatial tasks. Without any training, SpaceMCP achieves state-of-the-art results on VSI-Bench, MMSI-Bench and VSTI-Bench, outperforming recent spatially post-trained VLMs and improving its base VLM by 15-22 points per benchmark. We argue that, given the contrast between the relatively slow progress in native VLM spatial reasoning and the rapid advances in feed-forward 3D computer vision, this paradigm offers a promising path forward for enabling robust spatial reasoning.

</details>

Abstract: Parameterized-action MDPs (PAMDPs) ask an agent to choose a discrete action together with continuous parameters whose meaning depends on that choice. We introduce HyLI (Hybrid Latent Imagination), a model-based agent that learns a hybrid policy entirely in the latent imagination of a world model and acts with a single forward pass. To address learning problems that the PAMDP structure creates, HyLI adds counterfactual mode evaluation, which gives every discrete action a learning signal at every imagined state, and success-window replay, which keeps rare successes in training. On seven standard PAMDP benchmarks, HyLI is within one published standard deviation of, or above, the published score of the strongest model-free baseline in our comparison on all 7 tasks, reaches 90% of that score with 2.4x fewer environment steps than that baseline (5.0x when its reward-free pre-training is counted), and decides 67x faster than the model-based planner DLPA at its published planner settings. It is the only method in our comparison that is both more sample-efficient than that baseline and fast enough for real-time control.

Abstract: Bilevel optimization is a core tool in practical machine learning workflows, including hyperparameter tuning, data reweighting, robust learning, reinforcement learning, and imbalanced classification. Hypergradient computation requires the inverse of the inner Hessian, typically estimated via a truncated Neumann series. While existing analyses focus on bias and variance, we show that the product structure of the Neumann series introduces a third source of error: skewness. We identify that, unlike ordinary gradients, whose asymmetry is suppressed by mini-batch averaging, the Neumann estimator amplifies skewness across factors, with the third-moment error growing, under weak contraction, with truncation depth, inner condition number, and per-factor noise asymmetry. This arises in many practical settings such as corrupted labels, outliers, heterogeneous or class-imbalanced datasets. To address this, we introduce the Antithetic Neumann Estimator (ANE), which pairs each Hessian sample with a mirrored counterpart around a reference Hessian and averages the resulting inverse-Hessian estimates. With an exact reference, the antithetic product retains only even-order noise, eliminating all odd-order perturbation terms; with an approximate reference, the residual odd-order contribution is bounded linearly in the reference error. ANE requires only a modification of the Hessian vector step and combines with existing stochastic or variance-reduced bilevel methods. We instantiate ANE-SGD and ANE-VR, the latter adding recursive variance reduction. Across robust regression, data cleaning, offline reinforcement learning, and imbalanced classification, ANE-based methods improve on stochastic and variance-reduced baselines on every task (20 seeds each), lowering loss by 4 to 25% relative to the best baseline; the largest gains, on robust regression, are a 25% lower test loss and a 54% higher test r-squared.

</details>

Abstract: Imitation learning (IL) typically relies on large-scale offline demonstrations collected from multiple human or algorithmic demonstrators. However, most existing approaches assume these demonstrators are homogeneous or near-optimal experts, a convenient but unrealistic assumption in real-world applications. In practice, demonstrations are often collected from heterogeneous demonstrators with unknown and varying levels of expertise, resulting in highly mixed-quality data. In this work, we study a challenging yet practical setting of imitation learning from such heterogeneous imperfect demonstrators. We propose Latent Expertise and Optimality Scoring based Imitation Learning (LEOS-IL), a principled framework that jointly learns (i) a state-action optimality scoring model and (ii) latent expertise levels for each demonstrator from unlabeled suboptimal offline demonstrations. The learned scoring model is then integrated into an online policy optimization procedure, where the agent is trained to maximize estimated optimality scores while its online rollouts are iteratively leveraged to refine the scoring model, thereby mitigating distributional shift during online adaptation. We further provide theoretical guarantees for optimality score identification and conditional optimal occupancy and policy recovery under explicit sufficient conditions. Experiments on continuous control benchmarks demonstrate that our approach consistently outperforms existing baselines across highly heterogeneous and low-quality demonstration regimes.

</details>

Abstract: The growing volume of process data generated by screwdriving systems, combined with advancements in computing power, data storage, and algorithms, is facilitating the application of artificial intelligence in this field. Accurately detecting and classifying screwdriving errors is crucial for ensuring product quality, avoiding costly rework in manufacturing, and supporting sustainability by facilitating the maintenance, reuse, and recycling of screw-connected products. In this context, machine learning approaches offer a promising alternative to traditional monitoring techniques, such as window-based methods or envelope curves. This study examines the use of deep learning techniques for anomaly detection and error classification in screwdriving processes. The data corpus consists of multivariate time series capturing process parameters, such as torque and rotation angle. For anomaly detection, the embedding space of a vector quantized variational autoencoder was analyzed. In terms of error classification, four different model architectures were evaluated. The results demonstrate the effectiveness of anomaly detection and confirm that deep learning models can distinguish between multiple error categories beyond traditional binary classification. This extends the capabilities of conventional monitoring approaches, achieving accuracies of up to 100%. Additionally, real-world data material was examined in order to further demonstrate model robustness. Overall the results indicate strong generalization capability and highlight the practical potential of the developed models for use in industrial screwdriving processes, eliminating the need for expert knowledge in error identification and significantly reducing manual effort and analysis time.

Abstract: It is common to train Large Language Models (LLMs) for agentic tasks with Reinforcement Learning (RL) from scalar rewards. However, this throws away the fine-grained information about the environment that the agent experienced during the trial (e.g., error logs). To utilize this rich information, recent works wrap the agent in a harness that refines its own inputs through reflection or compaction and hands past experience to future rollouts, but the construction of the handed-off artifacts (e.g., a playbook) is itself not learned. We want the best of both worlds: gradient updates from a scalar reward, while also utilizing environment information to hand off experience. As a concrete testbed, we fix the Agentic Context Engineering (\ace{}) harness and train one model to act, reflect, and curate task-local playbooks. Our method, \acerl{}, evaluates candidate experience-handoff artifacts by their downstream effect on future actor rollouts, and co-optimizes the role-specific behaviors (action generator, insight reflector, and playbook curator) under a fixed scaffold. Across agentic benchmarks, \acerl{} achieves higher held-out performance than baseline methods. The results suggest that agent training should optimize not only the actions in the current rollout, but also the model-written experience that future rollouts inherit.

Abstract: To act effectively, agents must gather contextual information across resources such as chats, issue trackers, email and knowledge bases, to answer questions like "what was decided in the last project meeting?" or "where is the FAQ for our internal tooling?" Where this information lives in a user's environment is unknown to the agent, so it re-explores the same resources across consecutive interactions. Common routing approaches represent each resource as a tool and rely on high-level descriptions to generalize to unseen environments. Such descriptions often fail to separate similar or overlapping resources and cannot improve from the agent's subsequent interactions. We introduce Wayfinder, an adaptive router that uses the documents cited by the downstream agent as feedback about where relevant information resides. These citations update Wayfinder's internal resource representations, which subsequent interactions use to prioritize the resources likely to contain relevant information. We also introduce resource augmentation during offline reinforcement learning, which anonymizes and permutes resource identities to discourage training-time overfitting of which resource holds which topic; our ablations suggest it helps most when few training environments are available. Because no openly available professional environments pair realistic queries with resource-access patterns, we construct and release three controlled testbeds derived from existing datasets: NFCorpus, FEVER and Tech Startup. Compared with supervised, tool-retrieval and contextual bandit baselines under a shared closed-loop protocol, Wayfinder gives the best routing when resources contain semantically overlapping content that static descriptions cannot distinguish, while description-based tool retrieval suffices when resources are drawn from distinct corpora.

</details>
