Abstract: We present UniSpark-9B, a unified multimodal model that investigates hybrid attention for visual understanding and generation. Built on a Mixture-of-Transformer-Experts backbone with approximately 9B parameters in each branch, UniSpark combines full attention for explicit token interactions with GatedDeltaNet linear attention for compact context propagation. Autoregressive text prediction and visual flow matching jointly support visual question answering, text-to-image generation, instruction-guided editing, and interleaved text-image prediction. In interleaved generation, noisy visual targets are isolated from subsequent predictions, while clean visual representations provide context for later outputs. A blockwise protocol enforces this distinction through attention masks in full-attention layers and recurrent-state retention rules in linear-attention layers. The selected training sources contain 159.7M generation samples, 213.4M understanding samples, and 624.8K interleaved sequences. A four-stage curriculum jointly trains multimodal understanding and visual generation, refining answer accuracy and instruction following while extending image synthesis to 2K. UniSpark scores 2470.3 on MME-S and 0.83 on GenEval. Qualitative examples further illustrate its capabilities in interleaved image generation, video synthesis, and video editing. Together, these results demonstrate that hybrid attention provides an effective backbone for unified multimodal understanding and generation.

Abstract: 1. Monitoring plant biodiversity requires identifying large numbers of plants across space and time. Although artificial intelligence (AI) can now identify plant species from images with high accuracy, most existing systems rely on users manually submitting images. As ecological monitoring increasingly generates large image collections from cameras, drones, robots and online platforms, there is a growing need for plant identification services that can be directly integrated into automated data-processing workflows. 2. Here, we present Pl@ntNet's machine-to-machine AI plant identification service. This service uses an application programming interface (API) to provide programmatic access to Pl@ntNet's plant recognition models and the associated taxonomic processing. This service enables the implementation of automated identification workflows without human intervention and can thus directly link large image collections and automated observation systems to an AI-based plant identification system. 3. We establish that this service, which covers 85,000 species from around the world, (1) has a species prediction accuracy of 0.89, (2) process images up to 666 times faster than workflows requiring human intervention, (3) and has processed over 84 million identification requests since its launch, including more than 40 million in 2025 alone. We demonstrate how it has already enabled the implementation of a new generation of ecological workflows: identifying plants from images of insects to reconstruct the plant-pollinator interactions, analysing geolocated images from social media to study plant distribution, detecting invasive plants using images taken at the roadside, and identifying weeds using agricultural robots. Finally, we illustrate its application using a reproducible example: identifying plants from insect photos on iNaturalist. In approximately 30 min on a standard laptop, 2,424 images were processed to generate a dataset of 708 plant-pollinator interactions, with a weighted precision of 0.98 for flower detection and an accuracy of 0.88 for genus plant identification. 4. In conclusion, this machine-to-machine species identification is already operating on a considerable scale and meets a genuine need. All of this paves the way for a new paradigm in biodiversity monitoring, in which automated sensors and image collections can be directly connected to AI-based identification services.

Abstract: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) enable future prediction in latent space without pixel reconstruction. However, latent predictive consistency alone does not establish the relative driving quality of candidate plans. We present ActWorld-JEPA, a joint-embedding formulation for candidate-based driving. Where Plan-Conditioned JEPA (PC-JEPA) uses global and step-wise trajectory intentions to guide recurrent latent prediction, then combines the predicted future with the current scene through gated cross-attention; Relational Residual Reranking ($R^3$) models agreement and discrepancy between plan and future representations under planning supervision, yielding bounded, gated residual corrections to the base planning scores. This formulation connects latent prediction to candidate selection using only observed context and proposed trajectories at inference. Benchmark-specific implementations report 94.1 PDMS on NAVSIM v1 navtest, with the final selector calibrated using aggregate NAVTEST feedback, and 91.6 and 42.7 EPDMS on NAVSIM v2 navtest and navhard, respectively. The nuScenes evaluation reports a mean $L_2$ error of 1.20 m and a predicted collision rate of 0.76% over 1--6 s. This work provides an effective path from JEPA's predictive representations to driving decisions informed by anticipated consequences.

Abstract: Cameras and LiDARs (Light Detection and Ranging) provide complementary observations of the same scene: cameras capture rich appearance cues while LiDAR sensors provide accurate geometric measurements. In this work, we address the problem of fine image-to-point cloud registration from a single image and a roughly estimated camera pose. On robotic platforms, residual reprojection errors of several pixels are commonly observed even after calibration due to temporal desynchronization, mechanical vibrations, or inaccuracies in point cloud reconstruction. Such errors can lead to decimeter-level geometric discrepancies at moderate distances and significantly affect multiple downstream tasks, such as dense cross-modal supervision, texture mapping, and semantic label transfer. To address this challenge, we introduce ReFineR: an offline framework to finely register individual images to pre-reconstructed point clouds. ReFineR builds upon differentiable point splatting rasterization and a smooth Gradient Orientation Measure (GOM) to iteratively refine the camera pose through analysis-by-synthesis optimization. We evaluated the method on a curated dataset of manually calibrated images collected from heterogeneous data sources. Furthermore, we show that an automatic point cloud rescale factor can be estimated prior to optimization, enabling robust refinement across a wide range of scenarios using a single scale-invariant formulation. The project page can be found at https://refiner-project.github.io/

Abstract: Multimodal representation learning combines complementary signals to capture shared semantic information. To address this challenge, we propose MuViRL, a self-supervised framework for multimodal view-invariant visual speech representation learning via calibrated view aggregation. Abstract: Adapting general Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to Autonomous Driving (AD) is thought to require heavy 3D spatial priors or intrusive architectural overhauls to compensate for a lack of spatial grounding. In this work, we present a counterintuitive pilot study: off-the-shelf general VLMs already exhibit remarkable adaptability across diverse driving tasks, while precise metric depth remains the primary missing link, a bottleneck readily addressable via onboard sensors and depth foundation models. To this end, we introduce the Triplet Incremental Training Framework, a non-intrusive paradigm that seamlessly instills 3D geometric awareness into a general VLM backbone while fully preserving its open-world capabilities. Driven by automatically constructed (image, depth, QA pair) triplets, our framework operates via a unified tri-stream training pipeline: an unfrozen primary VLM stream to adapt to driving tasks, an auxiliary depth stream that guides the visual backbone to implicitly imagine 3D scene geometry via explicit geometric constraints, and a frozen twin VLM stream employing logit-level KL distillation to prevent semantic drift. Crucially, the auxiliary depth branch is discarded during deployment, ensuring the enhanced geometric awareness comes with zero inference overhead. We evaluate our framework across four major benchmarks targeting four diverse capabilities spanning 3D spatial perception, driving-oriented language reasoning, end-to-end planning, and general multimodal understanding. Our approach outperforms state-of-the-art driving VLMs, rivals domain-specific expert models in 3D perception, and successfully retains its original general reasoning capabilities. This demonstrates that Triplet Incremental Training offers a unified, deployment-ready paradigm for transforming general foundation models into robust driving intelligences.

</details>

Abstract: LiDAR is a core sensor for autonomous driving, yet it is highly vulnerable to fog. Fog scattering produces strong backscatter near the sensor and attenuates reflections from objects behind it, so weak object returns are often discarded by the peak detection that converts received signals into point clouds. Full-waveform LiDAR (FWL), which records the received optical signal before peak detection, retains these weak returns, but the lack of suitable data has prevented research on recovering fog-hidden objects from FWL. We present Fog-FWL, a dataset foundation for this task that consists of a real-world benchmark, Fog-FWL-Real, and a large-scale synthetic training set, Fog-FWL-Sim. Fog-FWL-Real contains 7,527 real foggy FWL frames from 8 scenes and 58 sequences, which is 579x larger than the only existing real foggy FWL data, together with histogram-level Object/Fog/Noise labels, 10,719 3D bounding boxes, and three difficulty levels defined by the attenuation of object reflections. Fog-FWL-Sim contains 12,000 frames generated by our physics-based simulator, Fog-FWL-Simulator, which integrates spatially heterogeneous fog density fields, time-resolved light transport including multiple scattering, and a photon-counting SPAD receiver model. The two subsets share the same sensor format and annotation protocol, enabling a strict sim-to-real evaluation in which no real waveforms are used for training. Our analysis shows that, under the hardest condition, object returns are included in the three strongest peaks for only 34.9% of object rays, underscoring the need for waveform-level recovery. Fog-FWL-Real, Fog-FWL-Sim, and the Fog-FWL-Simulator will be publicly released to facilitate research on fog-robust LiDAR perception.

Abstract: We present Kosmo-GFM, a scene reconstruction system that integrates a geometry foundation model (GFM), a pose optimization engine (PoseEngine), and reconstruction-derived supervision from KosmoWorld. GFM predicts camera parameters, depth, cross-view correspondences, and dynamic 3D points from images and videos. PoseEngine combines these predictions with geometric optimization to recover scene-level camera poses and geometry for downstream 3D and 4D Gaussian Splatting. KosmoWorld generates images and geometric supervision from reconstructed real-world scenes to improve GFM's geometric predictions. On the Sparse and Medium subsets of SpatialBench, GFM achieves the lowest absolute relative depth error and the highest relative-pose AUC at 5 degrees among the compared methods. It also achieves the lowest mean endpoint errors for dynamic points and scene flow across the five evaluated datasets. Across 34 scenes completed by all compared reconstruction systems, PoseEngine achieves the highest scene-averaged pose AUC at thresholds of 1, 3, and 5 degrees, with a cumulative runtime of 14.07 hours, approximately 20% of GLUEMAP's 69.67 hours. PoseEngine Flash provides a further accuracy-efficiency trade-off, with a measured mixed-precision runtime of 2.62 hours, approximately 4% of GLUEMAP's runtime. Separate scaling experiments show generally favorable trends with increased data, model computation, and training budgets. These results support the use of learned geometric priors within a scene-level reconstruction pipeline.

Abstract: While language-model agents excel at long-horizon coding and terminal tasks, this success relies on two conditions: explicit text specifications and immediate execution feedback. However, neither condition holds in real-world problems where critical information exists only in images, leaving it unclear whether long-horizon capability transfers from text to visual reasoning. In such visual settings, because rules and clues resist text transcription and lack test-case feedback, agents must repeatedly inspect images, simulate spatial transformations, and autonomously cross-check clues. Despite this challenge, existing evaluations fail to capture such capability: visual reasoning benchmarks largely reduce tasks to single-turn, short-chain question answering, whereas agent benchmarks focus on text-based environments or interactive states that permit trial-and-error. To bridge this gap, we introduce Visual Cipher Bench, a collection of 40 original visual puzzles across four categories: spatial manipulation and simulation, logical constraints and planning, rule induction, and knowledge-based decoding. Each puzzle provides only one to three black-and-white line drawings and a minimal submission instruction, requiring agents to discover the underlying solving mechanism entirely from the images. Across 29 reasoning-effort configurations of eight frontier models, even the strongest model, GPT-6 Astra (max), achieves only 26.7% accuracy, while five of the eight models score below 1%. Yet on a ten-task subset, providing a single non-spoiler mechanism hint boosts accuracy from 7.8% to 55.6%. This sharp contrast shows that the primary bottleneck of current agents lies not in executing multi-step plans, but in autonomously discovering rules from visual evidence. Visual Cipher Bench thus provides a rigorous benchmark for measuring long-horizon visual reasoning in agents.

</details>

Abstract: This report summarizes the Mobile Unified Multimodal Understanding (MUMU) track of the 8th Large-scale Video Object Segmentation (LSVOS) Challenge, held in conjunction with ECCV 2026. MUMU asks for a single unified model that jointly performs multi-concept image tagging over quality, scene, and event vocabularies, open-vocabulary object detection, and concise image captioning, within a budget of 0.5B parameters and 8 GB of peak inference memory. Submissions are ranked by a weighted task score scaled by an efficiency factor. We describe the tasks, rules, and evaluation protocol, report the final results, and review the methods of the top three teams. All three leading solutions build on the compact Florence-2-base model, but extend it in different ways: lightweight tagging heads with cross-task inference refinement, a shared low-rank adapter trained on coverage-aware multi-source data with task-dependent decoding, and a training-free pipeline that maps captions to tags and applies detection test-time augmentation. The results show that a compact prompt-based vision-language model can serve all three outputs when supervision, decoding, and cross-task cues are designed for each task, while open-vocabulary detection on novel categories remains the main bottleneck.

Abstract: Large foundation models such as GPT-6 Astra and Claude Fable 5.1 have recently been shown to control robots directly from visual observations. They nevertheless remain brittle on long-horizon, contact-rich tasks in real-world environments. Further progress depends on two complementary resources: demonstrations of human behavior that supervise how tasks are performed, and interactive environments in which policies can be rolled out, evaluated, and improved in closed loop. Language models have advanced along well-characterized scaling laws; the corresponding question for physical AI is how capability scales with environments and behaviors, which first requires identifying which data to scale and how to produce and reuse such data continuously. To address this question, we introduce Ropedia Xperience 2, comprising 1,589 released metric-scale 3D scans and a collection of 4,230 egocentric recordings of 74 tasks (131 hours). We further show that each capture can be repurposed for robot learning: scenes are converted into physically interactive simulation environments, human motion is retargeted to whole-body robot trajectories, and additional robot behaviors are obtained through virtual teleoperation and automated long-horizon trajectory generation. We propose this joint capture of environments and behaviors as a gold standard for physical AI data collection, laying a data foundation for establishing scaling laws over environments and behaviors.

Abstract: The proliferation of user-generated video content has made accurate and robust no-reference video quality assessment (NR-VQA) a highly important challenge. Existing research increasingly relies on architecturally complex multi-branch pipelines or computationally intensive fine-tuning of single-branch backbones. In this paper, we investigate whether a single frozen video foundation model can serve as an effective NR-VQA backbone, and propose HiVE-VQA, the first framework (to our knowledge) to apply a JEPA-based video encoder to this task. HiVE-VQA extracts token grids from multiple depths of a pre-trained V-JEPA 2.1 encoder and stacks them into a hierarchical spatio-temporal volume, which a compact 3D convolutional head fuses across its layer, spatial, and temporal axes, combining low-level distortion cues from shallow layers with the semantic and temporal context captured at greater depths. The encoder remains frozen throughout, so only the head is trained: 5.2M parameters (5.7\% of the total), with no auxiliary multi-modal models and an inference cost that is invariant to video resolution. Trained solely on LSVQ and evaluated without any fine-tuning, HiVE-VQA outperforms 17 state-of-the-art (SOTA) methods in SROCC and PLCC on both intra-dataset (LSVQ-test, LSVQ-1080p) and cross-dataset (KoNViD-1k, LIVE-VQC) benchmarks. These results indicate that hierarchical, depth-wise fusion of features from a single frozen video foundation model provides a more effective and efficient basis for NR-VQA than task-specific multi-network fusion. Code and model checkpoints are available at https://github.com/RobHam99/HiVE-VQA.

Abstract: Remote sensing change detection usually relies on large collections of temporally aligned, pixel-level annotations, which limits its scalability across regions, imaging platforms and application domains. Recent zero-shot approaches reduce the need for supervision, but they often localize fine-grained changes poorly because their features are coarse or inconsistent across time. We present SpotDiff, a training-free, plug-and-play framework for zero-shot change detection built by composing complementary vision foundation models. SpotDiff uses DINOv3 for semantically robust features, AnyUp to reconstruct them at pixel resolution, and SAM 3 for text-guided region-of-interest proposals, which an inference-time multi-scale strategy (SWIMS) makes reliable on small objects. Change is then measured by comparing the reconstructed features of the two dates inside the proposed regions, with no learnable components, fine-tuning or change labels. To our knowledge, SpotDiff is the first change detection method to use a universal feature upsampler, which alone adds 13.8 F1 over bilinear upsampling of the same features. On LEVIR-CD, SpotDiff reaches 82.46 F1, 2.06 points above the strongest prior training-free method, and on the multi-class SECOND benchmark it improves building change detection by 10.3 IoU and the six-class average by 2.2 IoU. Qualitative results on PlanetScope (3 m) and Sentinel-2 (10 m) imagery show that the same pipeline transfers across resolutions without modification. Our results indicate that composing modern foundation models is an effective route to scalable, label-free change detection.

Abstract: Differential morphing attack detection (D-MAD) compares a trusted live capture with a document image that may contain a facial morph. We investigate whether labelled validation data can improve a frozen-parameter vision-language evaluator through prompt selection rather than model fine-tuning. Version~1 generates 50 candidate prompts and selects the top-ranked instruction using a security-oriented loss that prioritises missed morph attacks (MACER) while accounting for bona fide classification errors (BSCER), response failures, parse failures, and decision inconsistency. Version~2 recursively revises prompts using aggregate validation feedback. On a subject-disjoint test set of 160 pairs spanning LMA, MIPGAN-2, and PIPE, the selected Version~1 prompt achieves a (19.17\%) morph attack classification error rate (MACER), with (0.00\%) observed bona fide sample classification error rate (BSCER), compared with (70.00\%) MACER for a controlled manual prompt. The paired (50.83) percentage-point difference has a (95\%) subject-bootstrap interval of ([43.33,58.33]). Recursive revision does not surpass the best Generation~0 validation loss; the reported revised descendant obtains (31.67\%) test MACER. All 23 attacks missed by Version~1 are landmark-based (LMA) morphs. Overall, the results show that prompt formulation can substantially influence frozen-parameter VLM-based D-MAD performance, while also highlighting the challenge of recursive prompt refinement under a compact 10-subject validation setting.

Abstract: Spatial intelligence requires connecting visual observations to the geometry, objects, and persistent state of an environment. Images alone leave these relationships implicit, while disconnected annotations make it difficult to study appearance, camera motion, and scene changes under a common reference. We introduce Spatial Foundry, a collection of 5,280 furnished residential 3D scene configurations with editable assets and aligned visual, geometric, and temporal data. Its modalities include floor plans, perspective and panoramic RGB, metric depth, point clouds, meshes, instance-level 3D bounding boxes, and camera trajectories paired with video. Shared scene coordinates link observations across views and time; editable source scenes enable additional camera paths and controlled object changes. We describe the data representation and construction workflow, and discuss applications in floor-plan-conditioned 3D generation, geometric reconstruction, instance-level perception, camera-conditioned video generation, shared world modeling, and scene editing. Controlled camera and scene variations support spatial perception and world-model research. The public project page provides videos and an interactive colored point-cloud preview.

Abstract: How multimodal language models should encode visual input remains up for debate. Most models use a pretrained vision encoder, while some recent recipes train the encoder from scratch with the language model or remove it entirely. Vision encoders bundle together three design choices: a visual prior from upstream pretraining, dedicated vision capacity, and pre-fusion placement of that capacity (i.e., before the multimodal backbone). Because published models also differ in data, architecture, and compute, comparing them cannot isolate the effect of each choice. We disentangle these choices via a controlled framework where we pretrain all models from scratch on common data and compare them on visual question answering and language benchmarks at matched lifetime compute. We find that the visual prior is not necessary; an encoder trained jointly from scratch is on par with a pretrained one, without a separate training stage. What matters instead is dedicated vision capacity: an encoder-free model trails on visual understanding even when its backbone is enlarged by the encoder's parameter count. Yet that capacity need not precede multimodal fusion, as vision-only modules added in-fusion to early backbone layers perform comparably to a pre-fusion encoder and can lower time to first token. These results also hold across backbone sizes, when encoder pretraining is treated as free, and when starting from a pretrained language model. Overall, our findings favor dedicated vision capacity trained jointly with the backbone and placed early within it.

</details>

Abstract: Classical Sanskrit poses three challenges unresolved by single-call monolithic models: morphophonemic fusion Sandhi, dense nominal compounding Samāsa, and discourse-level zero-anaphora forced by poetic meter. We present MITRA-Swarm, a multi-agent architecture injecting explicit linguistic structure into an otherwise monolithic pipeline: a Sandhi-splitting agent, a parallel array of generalist gemma-4-26B-A4B-it and domain-specialized gemma-2-mitra-it translators, and a discourse-aware Ellipsis Restorer that consults corpus-derived register tags to avoid inventing active-voice subjects in impersonal legal and philosophical registers. Two separated judges evaluate quality: a routing judge ($J_{\text{route}}$, same model family as the generators) driving an internal critique loop, and an independent reporting judge ($J_{\text{report}}=$gpt-4o-mini, no shared vendor lineage) scoring every output exactly once. On a stratified benchmark of 1,000 verses across five registers (23,990 scored verdicts, 12-condition pre-registered ablation), the full swarm reaches $85.90$ MQM against a monolithic baseline of $85.27$ ($Δ=+0.63$, $p_{\text{adj}}=0.0018$, 95% CI $[+0.30,+0.97]$), and isolated discourse restoration alone accounts for $+0.44$ MQM ($p_{\text{adj}}=0.0288$), outperforming a call-count-matched self-refinement baseline by $+0.51$ MQM ($p_{\text{adj}}=0.0288$). Severe per-verse failures (MQM$<80$) fall from $9.0\%$ to $7.6\%$, at roughly $9\times$ the per-verse inference cost of the baseline. Additionally, $J_{\text{route}}$ scores its own family's outputs $2$-$7$ MQM higher than $J_{\text{report}}$ on every one of the 12 conditions, empirically motivating the two-judge split; and a preflight screen of a 192,151-entry retrieval index against all 12 evaluation works verifies 0 reference leaks across 269 runtime checks. Abstract: Recent Vision-Language Models (VLMs) perform strongly on multimodal tasks, Document Understanding (DU) among them, but the benchmarks that measure them mostly cover English documents and narrow problems. How well current VLMs generalize across languages, tasks, styles and layouts therefore remains unclear. We introduce MDU, a multilingual DU benchmark of 23146 examples spanning 13 tasks and five languages: English, French, German, Italian, and Spanish. The tasks isolate complementary capabilities, from surface-level text and Markdown extraction through summarization, classification and named-entity recognition to layout-sensitive passage and layout extraction. We evaluate 45 state-of-the-art open-weight VLMs from 1.7B to 235B parameters, plus one commercial system under the same protocol. The benchmark is far from saturated: the best average is 69.0, only eight of the 45 evaluated systems reach 65, and the two best results come from 27B models rather than from the largest systems we evaluate. Performance is uneven in ways that scale does not fix. The gap between the strongest and weakest language does not close as models grow, and it survives controlling for the domain composition of each language. Capabilities also do not improve together: once we partial out overall model quality, per-task scores are largely uncorrelated, and transcription ability in particular trades off against semantic extraction.

Abstract: Full-duplex spoken dialogue, in which a system listens while it speaks and can backchannel, yield the floor, and be interrupted, is usually obtained by training on hundreds of thousands to millions of hours of speech. This is costly, rewrites the language model that supplies the system's knowledge, and still leaves turn-taking either implicit or decided coarsely rather than word by word. We show that a frozen, general-purpose multimodal LLM can instead be adapted into a word-level full-duplex system with little data. Our system is a thinker-talker cascade on an 80 ms frame clock. The thinker keeps the LLM frozen and trains only lightweight adapters and a few new token embeddings (97M parameters); at every frame it emits one token of agent text, with pauses, yields, and onsets represented as tokens of the same vocabulary and kept legal by a finite-state machine. A four-stage curriculum, each stage anchored to the model's own behavior under the previous format, performs the conversion in about 30 GPU-hours. The talker reads only the thinker's discrete tokens, so the two halves are trained independently. On Full-Duplex-Bench and InteractionBench, the system is competitive with open-source full-duplex systems built through large-scale speech training: among them, it is the least likely to take the floor when the user merely pauses, backchannels most often, and leads in spoken-task accuracy, groundedness, and coherence over long conversations.

</details>

Abstract: Intelligent fault diagnosis models are typically developed for a single machine under a specific set of operating conditions, such that each new machine or condition requires retraining from scratch. This one-machine-one-model paradigm scales poorly. Although transfer learning and domain generalization can reduce the labeling burden, they primarily seek domain-invariant commonality, potentially discarding machine-specific characteristics that are critical for diagnosis. This paper proposes TFC-MMFD, a time-frequency-condition multimodal fusion foundation model for rotating-machinery fault diagnosis under multi-element heterogeneous scenarios. Statistical indicators, time-frequency images, vibration sequences, and textual machine and condition descriptions are tokenized into a unified language-like space, allowing inputs of different physical origin to be represented as a single sequence. A LLaMA backbone, together with cross-modal bidirectional attention and a Q-Former, then distills this sequence into fault-mode queries. Diagnosis is performed by matching these queries against natural-language fault-mode anchors rather than fixed class indices, while regression-adversarial condition disentanglement and condition-conditioned masked pre-training improve robustness to changing conditions. Across four progressive tasks on ten public bearing datasets spanning diverse bearing types, speeds, and loads, TFC-MMFD attains 99.40% accuracy and 99.16% macro-F1, maintains over 80% accuracy on most interpolated unseen conditions without adaptation, and improves from 20.26% to 95.63% accuracy on an unseen machine with only five labeled samples per class. It outperforms the strongest of eight domain-generalization baselines by 10.57 percentage points and compares favorably with a recent bearing foundation model while using less than half of its fine-tuning data.

</details>

In each round, the deployed system serves a fresh batch of tasks; the base model diagnoses failed conversations and writes rules into the skills, and a rule survives only if it passes code checks, counterexample self-review, a paired retest, and a noise-calibrated validation gate. On the retail and airline domains of $τ^2$-bench, without training or selecting on official tasks, the agent after two rounds improves on this starting point by 12.5 pass$^1$ points in retail and 19.5 in airline, and by 18.4 and 22.0 pass$^4$ points. Abstract: In the legal domain, LLMs can improve reliability and interpretability by generating explicit reasoning traces. However, training LLMs to produce high-quality reasoning traces remains challenging. Existing supervised fine-tuning methods struggle when reasoning traces are significantly longer than final answers, as the learning signal for the answer becomes diluted. Meanwhile, reinforcement learning methods such as Group Relative Policy Optimization also have several limitations, including costly reward design, performance plateaus, and reward hacking. We therefore propose a two-stage training framework. In Stage I, JurisCoT-SFT employs a length-normalized dual objective to balance learning signals between reasoning traces and final answers. In Stage II, Lifecycle-Aware Backtrackable Policy Optimization dynamically evaluates and retires auxiliary reward signals based on their impact on final evaluation objectives, enabling efficient reward utilization. Our framework substantially improves legal reasoning performance on relevant tasks selected from multiple legal benchmarks, including ChJGBench, LawBench, and LegalBench. Experimental results demonstrate consistent improvements in both reasoning trace quality and final answer accuracy, while our models remain competitive with larger models.

Abstract: LLM-based recommendation agents can search catalogs, inspect products, ask users, and revise rejected recommendations. However, these behaviors are often governed by hand-designed workflows, while existing training methods optimize isolated components such as query rewriting or list ranking rather than the interaction policy as a whole. We present SimRec, a reinforcement learning framework that trains the full interaction policy of a tool-integrated recommendation agent. SimRec introduces two components. A task-anchored user simulator provides feedback on every recommendation: conditioned on the current task need rather than on user profile, and it can be an external LLM or the recommender itself under self-play. A task-aligned reward optimizes the whole trajectory with GRPO, crediting natural retrieval quality and valid tool use alongside the final hit, with training-only target injection to handle sparse hits over large catalogs. On ESCI and Amazon C4, SimRec improves the NDCG@100 of a Qwen3-4B agent from 32.23% to 35.42% and from 22.94% to 24.72%}, outperforming training-free agents on far larger backbones and task-aligned baselines on NDCG. Analysis shows that the trained agent verifies candidates, uses preferences, and recovers from rejected recommendations far more often than its untrained counterpart. Our code is available at https://github.com/ZJU-REAL/SimRec .

Abstract: Post-training can improve rewarded behavior while weakening capabilities users still need. We study two GRPO branches from a shared supervised checkpoint in a multilingual mixture-of-experts model. The released branch gains 36.07 points on MATH but loses 19.32 F1 points on XQuAD-Indic, while XQuAD-English improves under the same scoring rule. An independently trained sibling adds Indic instruction following and language-aware shaping. It recovers the regressing Indic task scores and preserves MATH, but does not restore script compliance and trades off competition mathematics and code. In a later round, judge training is followed by a sharp extraction decline from the Indic branch; reward-free distillation from its earlier checkpoint restores most of the lost F1. A MATH score decomposition associates most of the measured gain with increased boxed-answer emission; accuracy among boxed answers changes little. These results show why benchmark scores should be read alongside the response forms they reward.

Abstract: We present Fikiria, a two-stage SFT+GRPO recipe for Swahili mathematical reasoning. Its reward multiplies a format gate by a weighted sum of answer accuracy and the ChrF\texttt{++} similarity of the Swahili reasoning to an in-language reference chain of thought, so that training targets the surface fidelity of the reasoning trace and not only the final answer. We train on a 2.3k-problem Swahili dataset created by translating GSM8K with GPT-4.1 and filtering with SSA-COMET. On AfriMGSM Swahili, Fikiria-AfriqueQwen-14B reaches 79.2\%. Under the same fixed prompt and strict answer-format scoring, GPT-4o reaches 68.0\%, partly because it often fails the required answer format; IrokoBench reports 78.8\% for GPT-4o using a best-per-model prompt, a figure numerically close to ours but not directly comparable. In a small human evaluation with co-author annotators, GPT-4o's outputs were preferred overall. ChrF\texttt{++} measures surface fidelity, not the validity of individual reasoning steps.

Abstract: We present EvenVLM, the first text and multimodal (VQA) dataset for Even (Lamut), an endangered Northern Tungusic language spoken by approximately 6,800 people in Siberia. Our dataset comprises 50,521 structured Even-Russian entries extracted from dictionary and phrasebook sources via GPT-4o Mini-based parsing, from which we synthetically generate 107,543 training examples spanning translation, grammar, reading comprehension, and semantic classification. To address tokenization inefficiency on Even's agglutinative morphology, we propose a three-step tokenizer expansion algorithm that adds Even-specific graphemes (eng, oe, o-umlaut), whitelisted vocabulary, and BPE merges, reducing average tokens per word by 27% (4.99 to 3.64). Using LoRA fine-tuning of Qwen3-VL-4B, we adapt the model for text tasks (0.53% trainable parameters, rank 32) and separately for visual question answering (rank 64, frozen vision encoder). Our best configuration achieves 11.4% exact match and a 13.6 weighted score on text benchmarks, 0.1283 chrF for sentence translation, and 65% accuracy on VQA (up from 0% for the base model and text-only fine-tuning). We release our datasets, evaluation benchmarks, and training pipeline to support further research on Even and other low-resource languages.

</details>

Abstract: To keep long interaction contexts tractable, stepwise group-relative training for multi-turn LLM agents materializes each source trajectory into multiple response rows and valid tokens. This expansion creates an outer-accounting problem distinct from within-trajectory credit assignment: row-based outcome statistics can count one episode outcome multiple times, while token-level reduction can assign unequal aggregate objective mass across trajectories. We term these effects outcome-statistics multiplicity and objective-mass imbalance. We introduce Outcome-Mass Alignment (OMA), a two-component method that makes both accounting choices explicit. Outcome Alignment computes outcome statistics over unique source trajectories, while Mass Alignment controls aggregate objective mass after within-trajectory aggregation of optimizer-native token objectives. Neither redesigns the base optimizer's credit assignment, importance ratios, or clipping. Across matched ALFWorld GRPO comparisons at two model scales, where failed trajectories typically expand much more than successful ones, OMA improves matched-horizon endpoint success by 7.0-9.4 percentage points. Ordinary WebShop exhibits weak or reversed expansion-outcome coupling and correspondingly mixed effects, whereas in a success-oriented WebShop setting, WebShop-SOL, OMA improves success nAUC from 0.399 to 0.511. Component ablations and GiGPO and GSPO comparisons further support component separability and cross-optimizer compatibility. These results show that trajectory expansion always defines an implicit weighting rule, but its practical effect depends on how expansion couples to outcomes and optimizer-native updates.

</details>

Abstract: Remasking allows diffusion language models to revisit generated text, but effective correction requires knowing when and what to revise. Our diagnosis on Sudoku reveals two gaps. Hidden states contain error information that token confidence does not reliably expose, and remasking an isolated error usually reproduces it when the surrounding context remains fixed. These findings motivate DiVR , a verifier that predicts whether a partially generated sequence will reach a correct answer. Trained on the final correctness of the model's own trajectories, DiVR decides when to regenerate a block and which alternative to retain, while the generator itself stays unchanged. On five reasoning benchmarks with LLaDA-8B-Base, DiVR raises the average accuracy of the fine-tuned generator from 35.3\% to 43.4\% at $1.9$-$3.5\times$ the cost of greedy decoding, above search baselines that use $3.8$-$7.4\times$. It also complements post-training such as supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning, and generalizes to other generators, including Dream.

</details>

Abstract: This paper develops a distributed inverse-optimal fixed-time integral reinforcement learning framework for unknown nonlinear leader-follower multi-agent systems. The proposed method first reconstructs the unknown agent dynamics from finite integral data. Based on the identified dynamics, a distributed inverse-optimal formulation is constructed by prescribing a desired fixed-time value function and recovering a compatible running cost. A critic-only integral reinforcement learning architecture is then developed using current integral data and finitely informative experience-replay data, thereby avoiding the persistent-excitation requirement. Practical fixed-time convergence of the critic-weight errors is established in the presence of identification and function-approximation residuals. Finally, a network-level composite Lyapunov analysis proves practical fixed-time leader-following consensus of the true unknown nonlinear multi-agent system. Numerical simulations are provided to validate the developed theory.

</details>

Abstract: Real-time optimal power flow (RT-OPF) requires fast and reliable decision-making under time-varying operating conditions. However, many practical operating preferences and safety related requirements are difficult to encode using conventional numerical rewards alone, which limits the effectiveness and controllability of deep reinforcement learning (DRL) for RT-OPF. To address this issue, this paper proposes an LLM-enhanced DRL framework with human-in-the-loop (HITL) for RT-OPF. The proposed method augments the physics-based reward with sparse language evaluative feedback during offline training, so that semantic operational requirements can be incorporated into policy learning. To improve reliability in RT-OPF, a selective HITL calibration mechanism is further introduced to inspect and correct questionable LLM judgments before they continuously shape the learning process. Case studies on the IEEE 30-bus and IEEE 118-bus systems show that LLM feedback improves policy safety over vanilla TD3, while further HITL calibration achieves the best overall performance and more stable training behavior. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of combining language guidance and expert calibration for RT-OPF.

</details>

Abstract: Controllers designed from nominal models often degrade on mechanical systems with nonlinearities and parametric variations, such as automotive powertrains with backlash. Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) with domain randomization can acquire robustness to such model discrepancies, but wide randomization destabilizes training and yields conservative policies, and experimental transfer of such policies has rarely been demonstrated in vibration control. This paper presents model-based controller assisted domain randomization (MBCADR), a framework that integrates model-based control into domain-randomized DRL. An $H_{2}$ controller designed from the nominal model supplies a baseline action, while a residual policy trained with twin delayed deep deterministic policy gradient under randomized dynamics compensates for nonlinearities and uncertainties. This model-based assistance relieves the agent of synthesizing the entire control input, thereby stabilizing training under extensive randomization. For each realization of the uncertain parameters, the augmented plant--controller state is shown to be a Markov state of the closed loop, so that domain-randomized training is rigorously formulated as policy optimization over a parameterized family of Markov decision processes. A Lyapunov-based stability analysis further derives a forward-invariant set and an explicit ultimate bound, in which the learned action enters only through its saturation level. Monte Carlo simulations with multiple training seeds show that MBCADR reduces performance variability compared with either component alone. Experiments on a powertrain test bench with backlash, using the simulation-trained policy on an embedded processor without further tuning, confirm successful sim-to-real transfer.

</details>

Abstract: AI models of the power grid must capture how wind and solar plants support the voltage, adjusting their reactive power to the local voltage along a grid-code Q(V) characteristic. This research examines whether grid foundation models, fast neural network models for power-flow analysis, can represent this behaviour. We introduce the Q(V) bus, a new bus type for the open-source GridFM GraphKit, whose physics decoder evaluates the reactive injection from the plant characteristic at the predicted voltage in every correction step. Training data comprise 7882 hourly operating points of a modified IEEE 118-bus system, matched to Energinet DK1/DK2 measurements and solved with pandapower. Compared with a physics-informed baseline that represents the parks as negative load, the Q(V) bus lowers the voltage-magnitude RMSE 2.3-fold, reduces the power-balance mismatch at the parks from 25 to 0.66 MVA, and raises the F1 score for voltage-band violations from 0.40 to 0.76. An ablation over five configurations shows that the Q(V) bus type provides the accuracy and the physics loss provides the power balance. For edge monitoring, we distil the trained $2.0\times10^{7}$-parameter model into students of $6\times10^{5}$ and $1.3\times10^{6}$ parameters; a flat-start initialisation prevents their collapse into an incorrect low-voltage state in which the characteristic saturates. With output distillation, the 12-step student reaches 1.4 times the teacher's error and, for edge inference on an NVIDIA Jetson Orin Nano (15 W), solves an hourly scenario in 12.2 ms, within the 20 ms synchrophasor interval.

</details>

Abstract: We demonstrate the use of reinforcement meta-learning (meta-RL) to optimize a target jerk estimation policy, which is then used as the target acceleration rate of change in the propagation model of an unscented Kalman filter (UKF). The policy is learned through simulated engagements of a terminal phase high-altitude endoatmospheric interception of a highly maneuverable target, with a hit-to-kill accuracy requirement. As benchmarks, we tuned both a standard UKF and an interacting multiple model (IMM) filter, where the IMM uses a different constant jerk assumption for each model. The benchmarks and the proposed augmented UKF with the target jerk policy are tested in a 6-DOF simulation environment, which has sufficient fidelity to capture most factors relevant to guidance, navigation, and flight control. The augmented UKF demonstrates significant improvement in target interception rate, with more challenging maneuvers giving the largest performance differential. The meta-RL jerk policy is then tested on an expanded engagement scenario, with increased initial state estimate uncertainty and novel target maneuvers, demonstrating that the meta-RL policy generalizes well to conditions not seen during optimization.

Abstract: The rapid growth of distributed energy resources (DERs), including rooftop solar and energy storage, is transforming the grid edge, where customer-side assets increasingly interact with the broader power grid. DER aggregators, entities that coordinate the actions of many small-scale DERs, play a key role in this transformation. This paper presents a hybrid Mean-Field Control (MFC) and Mean-Field Game (MFG) framework for integrating DER aggregators into wholesale electricity markets. Unlike traditional approaches that treat market prices as exogenous, our model captures the feedback between aggregators' collective actions and electricity prices. The MFC component optimizes DER operations within each aggregator, while the MFG models strategic interactions among multiple aggregators. To account for various uncertainties, we incorporate reinforcement learning, which allows aggregators to learn optimal bidding strategies in dynamic market conditions. We prove the existence and uniqueness of a mean-field equilibrium and validate the framework through a case study of the Oahu Island power system. Results show that our approach reduces price volatility and improves market efficiency, offering a scalable and decentralized solution for DER integration in wholesale markets.

Abstract: Nonlinear time-optimal model predictive control (TOMPC) enables highly performant control of constrained systems, but its computational cost prohibits real-time use on fast dynamical systems. Neural network imitation of the TOMPC control law can reduce online computational costs, yet existing work mostly targets quadratic cost formulations in simulation and addresses the challenges of neural controllers in isolation. This work imitates a long-horizon nonconvex TOMPC with hard terminal constraints, whose high computational cost limits training-data generation. We present (i) \emph{self-informed sampling (SIFS)}, a data-efficient expert-sampling strategy that uses a learned time-to-go to query the expert only where the policy deviates from time-optimal behavior, (ii) a complete training and control architecture that jointly addresses dataset correlation, suboptimal expert labels, covariate shift, constraint satisfaction and model mismatch, and (iii) real-world validation on a Furuta pendulum. SIFS matches standard dataset aggregation performance in roughly one-third of the training time and with less than half the training data. The resulting controller matches the region of attraction of the expert TOMPC while raising the control frequency from 1.4~Hz to 1~kHz. On hardware, the controller swings up and stabilizes the pendulum along trajectories closely matching the expert TOMPC solution.

</details>
